<p> Ottawa Citizen - RSS Feedhttps : **28;17807;TOOLONG , 21 Jul 2020 23:32:55 +0000uOttawa team examines effects of oral contraceptives on brain developmenthttps : **28;17837;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;17867;TOOLONG ... title= " Prof. Nafissa Ismail poses for a photo at her home in Gatineau . Nafissa is one of the authors of a new study looking at how the use of oral contraceptives affects women during puberty and adolescence . " alt= " Prof. Nafissa Ismail poses for a photo at her home in Gatineau . Nafissa is one of the authors of a new study looking at how the use of oral contraceptives affects women during puberty and adolescence . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;17903;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1679151 " /&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 100% " height= " 100% " frameborder= " 0 " allowfullscreen= " true " src= " https : **34;17933;TOOLONG ... &gt; &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;p&gt; Women who start using oral contraceptives during puberty or adolescence may not respond to stress as well as their counterparts who begin using birth-control pills as adults , or not at all , according to research done by the University of Ottawa . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Additionally , the study of 75 women suggests that early use of birth control pills may contribute to the development of mood-related disorders , such as depression . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Nafissa Ismail , associate psychology professor and uOttawa research chair in stress and mental health , and Dr. Andra Smith , uOttawa psychology professor , are leading a research team headed by graduate student Rupali Sharma . Their goal is to determine how the synthetic hormones in birth control pills affect women&amp;rsquo ; s health , particularly in young women whose brains are still in critical stages of development . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Using stress tests and magnetic resonance imaging , their research shows that women who use oral contraceptives displayed greater activity in their prefrontal cortices when performing memory tasks . The implication of this increased activity , Ismail says , needs further investigation , but it may suggest that their brains simply have to work harder to arrive at the same outcomes as women who aren&amp;rsquo ; t on the pill . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The results of the first phase of their research are now being published in the journal Hormones and Behavior. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , the dulled response to social stress is of concern , she adds , because it suggests that the brains of early users of the pill have developed in ways that don&amp;rsquo ; t properly react to stress and thus never resolve it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The women who started contraception during puberty don&amp;rsquo ; t show that stress response that they should when they&amp;rsquo ; re presented with a stressor , &amp;rdquo ; she says . &amp;ldquo ; And that is worrisome because that means that , first , they&amp;rsquo ; re not reacting to the stressor , and secondly , they&amp;rsquo ; re not bringing their system back to homeostasis . So subjectively , they&amp;rsquo ; re telling us that they&amp;rsquo ; re stressed , but their body is not reacting to it . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And while any definitive causal relationship remains inconclusive , and women who were diagnosed with mental-health issues were excluded from the study , Ismail notes that a blunted stress response is common in women who are suffering from depression . &amp;ldquo ; It points to a mechanism that could explain why many women suffer depression once they start oral contraceptives. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Relatively little research has been devoted to the effects of oral contraceptives on neural development , despite the six decades that have passed since the pill came on the market . &amp;ldquo ; Women&amp;rsquo ; s health research has been lacking because we&amp;rsquo ; ve given priority to issues related more to men&amp;rsquo ; s health , or those affecting most of the population , and we&amp;rsquo ; ve kind of forgotten that 50 per cent of the population has unique problems , &amp;rdquo ; says Ismail . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; But it&amp;rsquo ; s worth investigation , and it needs investigation . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Oral contraceptives , which inhibit ovulation , help prevent sperm from entering the fallopian tubes and deter any fertilized eggs from attaching to the uterine wall , are used by an estimated 150 million women . They are also prescribed to young women to combat acne problems , heavy menstrual flow and pre-menstrual syndrome . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We want to find out the impact that it has on these young women , too , &amp;rdquo ; says Ismail . &amp;ldquo ; And we want to find the mechanism that would help us understand why some women are more susceptible than others to developing mood disorders , and whether the birth-control pill is a contributing factor , and if there is any way to predict , before a woman even starts to take the pill , whether it will be the right fit for her , or if there might be injuring consequences that we could prevent by using an alternate method . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We don&amp;rsquo ; t want to discourage women or girls from taking oral contraceptives , &amp;rdquo ; she adds . &amp;ldquo ; We just want them to be informed , to be able to weigh the pros and cons , and then make an informed decision . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **30;17969;TOOLONG " **37;18001;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; Bruce DeachmanTue , 21 Jul 2020 23:32:55 **45;18040;TOOLONG ... To reduce gender-based violence . &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;18216;TOOLONG ... six days&lt;/a&gt; , a woman in Canada is murdered by her intimate partner . &amp;nbsp ; Every night , women and children fleeing domestic violence sleep in shelters in Ottawa . To fight the climate crisis&lt;/strong&gt; . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Globally , 80 per cent of people displaced by climate change are women . Meeting Canada&amp;rsquo ; s ambitious climate target&amp;nbsp ; of&amp;nbsp ; net-zero carbon emissions by 2050 will not be accomplished if girls and women are not full participants in this effort . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Tapping in to the potential talent of half the population is imperative to accelerate clean tech innovation and growth , increase sustainable investments and policy making , and&amp;nbsp ; motivate&amp;nbsp ; individuals to reduce their carbon footprints. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;18286;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;18328;TOOLONG ... In Ottawa and elsewhere , there 's still work ahead to get women into **29;18363;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;4 ) . To protect women 's right to choose . &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Access to a full range of reproductive health technologies is a human right and supports a woman&amp;rsquo ; s health and wellbeing . However , &amp;nbsp ; in most countries , &amp;nbsp ; the ability to access safe and legal abortions is restricted in law or in practice . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Even in Canada , where abortion is permitted , some women experience limited access to safe abortion services because of a lack of health services or political will . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;5 ) . To promote women 's health . &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The medical research gender gap is real . Women are often excluded from clinical trials , limiting knowledge of the difference between women&amp;rsquo ; s health and men&amp;rsquo ; s , and putting women at increased risk from lack of prevention , diagnosis and treatment . For example , more than 30 authors of a recent review by Heart &amp;amp ; Stroke and the Canadian Women&amp;rsquo ; s Heart Health Alliance termed the &lt;a href= " https : **33;18394;TOOLONG ... of research&lt;/a&gt; into women&amp;rsquo ; s cardiovascular disease &amp;ldquo ; stunning . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;span style= " font-family : -apple-system , BlinkMacSystemFont , ' Segoe UI ' , Roboto , Oxygen-Sans , Ubuntu , Cantarell , ' Helvetica Neue ' , sans-serif ; " &gt;They highlighted that women are under-diagnosed , under-treated and less aware of the risks they face from cardiovascular disease , which is the number one killer of women . &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;6 ) . To walk in solidarity with Indigenous women . &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Indigenous women are assaulted almost three times more often than non-Indigenous women . &amp;nbsp ; Despite representing only five per cent of the population , Indigenous women now represent &lt;a href= " https : **33;18429;TOOLONG ... per cent of incarcerated women&lt;/a&gt; in Canada&amp;rsquo ; s prisons . &lt;a href= " https : **32;18464;TOOLONG ... than 80 per cent&lt;/a&gt; of First Nations women living on reserves have a median income below the poverty line . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;7 ) . To boost the number of women in politics . &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;18498;TOOLONG ... shows that&lt;/a&gt; , as more women are elected to public office , there is a corresponding increase in policymaking that emphasizes quality of life and reflects the priorities of families , women , and ethnic and racial minorities . And yet , progress to elect more women at all levels of government in Canada remains extremely slow , &amp;nbsp ; particularly&amp;nbsp ; for women of colour and Indigenous women . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The 2019 federal election saw 98 women elected , a modest gain of 10 in terms of representation from the 2015 election &amp;ndash ; from 26 per cent to 29 per cent of the 338 seats in the House of Commons . &amp;nbsp ; Of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s 13 first ministers , Caroline Cochrane of the Northwest Territories is the only woman . Municipally , just seven of 24 members of Ottawa Council are women . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Saturday&amp;rsquo ; s Women&amp;rsquo ; s March is an empowering and energizing opportunity to join diverse women and their allies to say that women&amp;rsquo ; s equality is not up for debate . It&amp;rsquo ; s also a rallying cry to elect more women for concrete progress on all these issues . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **29;18533;TOOLONG King&lt;/strong&gt; is a communications professional and ardent volunteer to elect more women to public office . Scientists have looked at these five treatmentshttp : **30;18745;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;18777;TOOLONG ... title= " " Gene Editing . " " alt= " " Gene Editing . " " data-portal-copyright= " Mike Faille " **28;18813;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1546798 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Scientists have been researching ways to increase a human&amp;rsquo ; s healthspan &amp;mdash ; and possible lifespan &amp;mdash ; for decades . Here are things they have considered , and what they have learned . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Rapamycin &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt; What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; First identified in the 1970s in a soil sample from Easter Island , this FDA-approved drug is used to suppress the immune system in transplant patients . Counterintuitively , it also appears to boost immune function in healthy animals . Rapamycin may reduce inflammation , which increases with age . One study found that rapamycin rejuvenated the hearts of old mice . After treatment , there were significant improvements to cardiac function . Research with mice has also found that even when they where administered rapamycin late in life , the mice still had improvements to lifespan and their healthspan. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict : &lt;/strong&gt; Geroscientist Matt Kaeberlein said rapamycin shows the most promise of any drug that targets aging , in his opinion , although he doesn&amp;rsquo ; t necessarily think rapamycin will increase lifespan by 20 to 30 years on average in people . &amp;ldquo ; Although it might at the right dose . I do think there&amp;rsquo ; s reason to believe that rapamycin could improve several age-related health problems , including boosting immune function and protecting against dementia , cancers , and heart disease . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But critics say it is unclear what the downstream effects could be . &amp;ldquo ; If you take a compound like that for a few decades , there may be downstream effect&amp;nbsp ; &amp;mdash ; a small epidemic of lymphoma , perhaps , &amp;rdquo ; said geriatrician Dr. Peter Boling . While it is approved to prevent rejection in organ transplant patients , it is not approved to treat aging . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are also questions about side effects , which may include susceptibility to infection and the possibility of developing lymphoma. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We still don&amp;rsquo ; t know about all of the hidden dangers . As hopeful as we might be , we have to be aware of the limitations , &amp;rdquo ; said Boling. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Metformin &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; This drug has been prescribed to millions of people to treat diabetes . Among other effects , it decreases insulin levels . It also appears to target biological mechanisms related to aging . Its anti-aging effects may be linked to its influences on metabolic and cellular processes associated with inflammation . It has been linked to lifespan extension and protection against age-related diseases in animal models . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict : &lt;/strong&gt; The speculation in scientific circles has been that many of the drugs used to treat early-stage chronic disease may be effective , at least in part , because they target aging itself . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Metformin is an interesting case , &amp;rdquo ; said Kaeberlein . It clearly increases survival in people with diabetes and likely would do the same for pre-diabetics and others with significant metabolic disease , which represents a growing percentage of adults , he said . However , it&amp;rsquo ; s less clear whether metformin will be beneficial in people who are metabolically healthy and exercise regularly . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The studies in mice suggest that metformin is different from rapamycin , in the sense that metformin only increases lifespan by a very small amount and in some cases has been reported to have no effect or even shortens lifespan in mice . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The FDA has accepted a clinical trial , Targeting Aging with Metformin , known as TAME , which will test whether metformin can delay the onset of age-related diseases and conditions including cancer , cardiovascular disease and Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s disease . The goal of the trial , which is estimated to cost $55 million , is not to help people live to be 120 years old but &amp;ldquo ; to add health to our years , &amp;rdquo ; said the American Federation for Aging Research . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Like metformin , several promising drugs show great potential and await trials . If successful , the TAME trial would give the pharmaceutical industry impetus to advance these drugs and transform aging from a period of sickness to a time of extended vitality . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Caloric restriction &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; A dietary regimen that reduces calories while maintaining nutrients . Research in lab animals , which started almost a century ago , has shown that it is possible to delay age-related diseases in mice and rats through dietary and caloric restriction , but results have varied . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A parallel study of two groups of rhesus monkeys , one at the University of Wisconsin and the other at the U.S. National Institute on Aging , looked at the life-prolonging and health effects of a calorie-restricted diet . The researchers found that the Wisconsin monkeys on a diet with 30 per cent fewer calories than the control group survived to about 28 years for males and about 30 years for females &amp;mdash ; a couple of years above average for monkeys in captivity . Calorie-restricted monkeys in both groups showed fewer age-related health conditions compared to the control monkeys . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some &amp;ldquo ; biohackers&amp;rdquo ; &amp;mdash ; people who use biological interventions to optimize health &amp;mdash ; have embarked on caloric restriction diets , with the goal of living longer . Entrepreneur Dave Asprey , author of Bulletproof Diet , has said he plans to live to be 180 and is hacking his own biology with a number of interventions , including supplements and a strict diet . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict so far : &lt;/strong&gt; The rhesus monkey study also produced some contradictory findings . The National Institute on Aging&amp;rsquo ; s study found that while the dieting monkeys showed improved health , they didn&amp;rsquo ; t necessarily live longer than the control monkeys . It&amp;rsquo ; s not clear why there was a difference between the two groups of monkeys , although they were fed different diets and had different feeding times and access to food . The ages at which they dieted may also have come into play . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There is no compelling evidence caloric restriction will work with people , &amp;rdquo ; said Doug Gray , a molecular biologist with an interest in the role of protein homeostasis in aging and diseases of aging at the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; If this sounds like a depressing route to extreme longevity , even scientists agree . Noted one researcher : &amp;ldquo ; Life might seem longer , but it wouldn&amp;rsquo ; t necessarily be longer . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Gene editing &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;span class= " ILfuVd " &gt;&lt;span class= " e24Kjd " &gt;Genome editing , or gene editing , is a type of genetic engineering in which DNA is inserted , deleted , modified or **34;18843;TOOLONG . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Last February , for example , the journal Nature Medicine reported on a therapy using &lt;span class= " ILfuVd " &gt;&lt;span class= " e24Kjd " &gt;CRISPR-Cas9 genome editing technology aimed at **26;18879;TOOLONG suppressing rapid aging in mice with the rare genetic disorder progeria , which causes accelerated aging . According to the report , two months after the therapy , the progeria mice were stronger and more active and had better heart health . Their lifespan also increased by about 25 per cent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict so far : &lt;/strong&gt; Not ready for prime time , said Boling . Even though we have successfully mapped the human genome , and it&amp;rsquo ; s technically possible to remove a gene sequence and replace it , we have yet to successfully edit genes in a complex organism . Many people don&amp;rsquo ; t want to eat food containing genetically-modified organisms , let alone become one , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I don&amp;rsquo ; t think we have a clear handle on a special gene sequence that would essentially change the aging process . I would want to see if we could do it in a limited and focused way without undue downstream consequences . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;18907;TOOLONG ... title= " In 2017 , researchers at Stanford University medical school reported that injections of blood plasma from donors between the ages of 18 and 30 showed promise for helping human Alzheimer 's patients with mild to moderate symptoms regain some ability to perform basic daily tasks . " alt= " In 2017 , researchers at Stanford University medical school reported that injections of blood plasma from donors between the ages of 18 and 30 showed promise for helping human Alzheimer 's patients with mild to moderate symptoms regain some ability to perform basic daily tasks . " data-portal-copyright= " Colleen De Neve Colleen De Neve " **28;18943;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1546888 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The intervention : &lt;/strong&gt; Young blood &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;What is it ? &lt;/strong&gt; There have been a number of studies that looked at whether giving old mice blood from young mice could reverse some signs of aging . Even the researchers admitted it sounded creepy , but the results were intriguing . One study at the University of California , Berkeley , published in 2018 , found that &lt;span class= " jsx-671803276 " &gt;old mice showed benefits , including better muscle repair , from receiving blood from young mice . But young mice who received old blood had negative effects , including performing poorly on a strength test . &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2017 , researchers at Stanford University medical school reported that injections of blood plasma from donors between the ages of 18 and 30 showed promise for helping human Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s patients with mild to moderate symptoms regain some ability to perform basic daily tasks , such as remembering to take their medications or preparing meals . The results were based on reports from the patients and their caregivers . The researchers cautioned that more research is needed , since the trial involved only nine patients who had received plasma infusions and nine who had received a placebo saline infusion. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;The verdict so far : &lt;/strong&gt; Don&amp;rsquo ; t try it . In a statement last February , the FDA said it had &amp;ldquo ; significant public health concerns&amp;rdquo ; about establishments that offer infusions of plasma from young donors to treat conditions ranging from normal aging and memory loss to Parkinson&amp;rsquo ; s disease , multiple sclerosis , Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s disease , and PTSD. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Simply put , we&amp;rsquo ; re concerned that some patients are being preyed upon by unscrupulous actors touting treatments of plasma from young donors as cures and remedies . Such treatments have no proven clinical benefits for the uses for which these clinics are advertising them and are potentially harmful , &amp;rdquo ; warned the FDA. &amp;ldquo ; There are reports of bad actors charging thousands of dollars for infusions that are unproven and not guided by evidence from adequate and well-controlled trials . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;18973;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;19015;TOOLONG ... this rare disease could help treat aspects of **26;19050;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;19078;TOOLONG ... would you do with 20 extra years ? Scientists are close to extending a human 's ' healthy lifespan ' &lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; Joanne LauciusThu , 30 Jan 2020 13:17:45 **45;19113;TOOLONG ... shortages will continue without innovative solutions : drug policy experthttp : **34;19160;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;19196;TOOLONG ... title= " Jacalyn Duffin , Queen 's University in Kingston , ON " alt= " Jacalyn Duffin , Queen 's University in Kingston , ON " data-portal-copyright= " Lars Hagberg " **28;19232;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1516922 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Shortages of drugs that treat breast cancer and childhood leukemia have gotten much of the attention lately , but they represent just a small fraction of all the drug shortages in Canada right now . And such shortages will continue unless dramatic changes are made , says a Canadian drug policy expert . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Jacalyn Duffin , professor emerita at Queens University who held the Hannah Chair for the History of Medicine until 2017 , said the federal government has to do a better job of measuring and understanding the cause and the scope of drug shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We can not talk about the drug shortage problem until we know what it is and how it changes over time . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2018 , Duffin and colleagues produced a report published by the C.D. Howe Institute that assessed Canada&amp;rsquo ; s drug shortage problem . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At the time , they recommended Health Canada provide annual reports on drug shortages in an effort to &amp;ldquo ; define it , explain it and help provide solutions to mitigate the risks associated with the shortages . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The authors also recommended Health Canada measured the medical necessity of drugs to help prioritize solutions to shortages and that Canada encourage increased self-reliance in the manufacture of drugs and active ingredients in drugs &amp;mdash ; meaning produce its own . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In an interview , Duffin noted that Health Canada is still not measuring and analyzing drug shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In an update on tamoxifen shortages , Health Canada said it assesses the severity and potential impact of drug shortages when they occur . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Many shortages can be resolved through supply management measures without their effect ever being felt by patients . Health Canada works closely with the Canadian companies that supply drugs , the provinces and territories , and health stakeholders across the supply chain to identify and facilitate mitigation measures , as needed . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Along with a better understanding of the scope of ongoing shortages , she said , Canada should consider making its own generic drugs and drug ingredients as a buffer against some shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It blows me away that in a country like Canada with intellectual capital and human resources , we don&amp;rsquo ; t just start making them . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;19262;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;19304;TOOLONG ... of reach : When cancer treatment is available but not **31;19339;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; In the U.S. , a group of hospitals and medical insurance groups formed a non-profit generic drug manufacturing company out of frustration over continuing shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada has a mandatory drug shortage and discontinuation database , drugshortagescanada.ca , which is operated by Bell Canada under contract with Health Canada . According to the website , there are currently 1,966 drug shortages and 52 anticipated drug shortages in Canada . The site lists , but does not analyze the shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Among drugs that continue to face shortages are tamoxifen , commonly prescribed for women with estrogen positive breast cancer . For weeks , patients have reported difficulty filling their prescriptions . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Apotex , which is the country&amp;rsquo ; s biggest supplier of tamoxifen , expects to have supply available &amp;ldquo ; as soon as possible , &amp;rdquo ; said Jordan Burman , vice-president of global corporate communications . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The company had initially said the drug would be available by the end of January , but after working with Health Canada has since moved that up twice , said Burman . He said he expects the shortage to end before Dec. 31 , its current target . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He said the shortage was due to a change in the manufacturing and formulation process . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We recognize the emotional burden it creates for patients and that is why we have accelerated the time line twice . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Vincristine , a chemotherapy used to treat childhood leukemia is also in short supply as are other cancer treatments . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At CHEO , children have had to miss treatments because of the shortage of the leukemia drug . Shortages are expected to continue until at least the end of January . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In a recent blog , Duffin said the reasons for drug shortages are complex and remain poorly understood . But she noted that numerous companies have stopped making some generic drugs , because the prices are so low . In 1996 , there were at least eight companies supplying tamoxifen in Canada . Now there are three . That has likely accelerated shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; When there are only a few makers , the margin in the system is narrow . Any manufacturing problem will result in shortage , &amp;rdquo ; she wrote . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We as a society accept the notion that drug marketing and drug development belongs in the private sector . These private companies are not charities ; if they are losing money , they stop making the drug so that they can keep paying their employees and their stakeholders . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; epayne@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;19372;TOOLONG ... behind facial deformity in one of history 's largest royal dynasties , study says&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;19407;TOOLONG ... showing Trudeau seemingly talking candidly about Trump goes viral&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;19442;TOOLONG ... Transpo crash victim 's widow speaks for first time of her mistreatment , heartache&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneWed , 04 Dec 2019 20:12:55 **45;19477;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal NewsMenNationalWomen'The bodies are piling up ' : Inside the opioid epidemic and the crisis that wo n't go awayhttp : **36;19524;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;19562;TOOLONG ... title= " Lorraine Lefebvre poses for a photo in Ottawa Friday , Nov. 15 , 2019 . Lorraine lost her daughter , Melony , to an opioid overdose in 2016. " alt= " Lorraine Lefebvre poses for a photo in Ottawa Friday , Nov. 15 , 2019 . Lorraine lost her daughter , Melony , to an opioid overdose in 2016. " data-portal-copyright= " TONY CALDWELL " **28;19598;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509668 " /&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 100% " height= " 100% " frameborder= " 0 " allowfullscreen= " true " src= " https : **33;19628;TOOLONG ... &gt; &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;p&gt; On the morning of Saturday , June 4 , 2016 , Lorraine Lefebvre woke up at around 5:30 , shortly after sunrise and an hour earlier than she&amp;rsquo ; d planned . She went to the bathroom to get a glass of water , but when she pushed against the door , it wouldn&amp;rsquo ; t open . She thought that perhaps her daughter Melony&amp;rsquo ; s boyfriend , who often stayed over on weekends , was passed out on the floor , so she went back to bed for another hour before she had to get up to get ready for work . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Lorraine had only been in Ottawa for six weeks , after driving from Alberta and reconnecting with Melony , 42 , whom she&amp;rsquo ; d seen fewer than a handful of times since moving out west in 2000 . Lorraine and her partner , James , were staying with Melony and her seven-year-old daughter &amp;mdash ; the only one of Melony&amp;rsquo ; s four children in her care &amp;mdash ; in their Britannia/Lincoln Heights home as they began new lives here . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Melony was really happy to see me , &amp;rdquo ; says Lorraine . &amp;ldquo ; She talked about getting a house together . She&amp;rsquo ; d never had a house ; it had always been apartments . And we would live happily ever after . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But happily ever after would not be that simple . Melony&amp;rsquo ; s life had been challenging , to say the least , with harms from trauma she experienced at a very young age setting the table for a chaotic and deeply troubled future . Sexually abused when she was young , she&amp;rsquo ; d been using drugs &amp;mdash ; heroin , cocaine , alcohol &amp;mdash ; since she was a teen , sometimes finding herself in , but mostly out of , rehab , including a six-week stint in Minneapolis when she was 15 . She returned home from that treatment just before Christmas , and the following month , just before her 16th birthday , ran away from home . She was soon robbing banks , dealing drugs and moving stolen goods that the crowd she hung out with supplied . &amp;ldquo ; She was very street smart , &amp;rdquo ; her mother recalls , &amp;ldquo ; and she learned fast how to manipulate the situation . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;19663;TOOLONG ... title= " Lorraine Lefebvre 's daughter , Melony , who died of an opioid overdose in 2016. " alt= " Lorraine Lefebvre 's daughter , Melony , who died of an opioid overdose in 2016. " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;19699;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509081 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Melony was also sweet and fun-loving , and loved to laugh . &amp;ldquo ; But you put that street drug into her and she became the total opposite , &amp;rdquo ; says Lorraine . &amp;ldquo ; She would fight anybody . She didn&amp;rsquo ; t care . She was a tough cookie . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But what is hope if not the promise of another chance , and , if necessary , another , and another after that ? Melony celebrated her mother&amp;rsquo ; s arrival in Ottawa by getting clean . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; She had hopes and dreams , &amp;rdquo ; says Lorraine . &amp;ldquo ; She was pretty excited . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Melony lasted two weeks before slipping back into the darkness of her substance use . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When Lorraine returned to the bathroom an hour later that Saturday morning , her entry was still blocked . She pushed a little harder this time , and caught a glimpse of Melony&amp;rsquo ; s nightie . Lorraine called out to James , who rushed to the bathroom and began performing CPR while Lorraine called 911 . Melony&amp;rsquo ; s face was purple and distorted , but her torso was warm , and Lorraine desperately hoped her daughter was still alive . While on the phone with the 911 operator , she heard Melony gasp for breath . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But the breath she thought she heard was simply gas being dispelled by James&amp;rsquo ; s efforts to resuscitate Melony . James just looked at Lorraine , unable to speak the words , to tell Lorraine that Melony was gone . Melony&amp;rsquo ; s young daughter , meanwhile , had awoken , and was curious to learn what all the commotion was about . Lorraine called out to Melony&amp;rsquo ; s boyfriend , who came upstairs and tried slapping Melony awake . &amp;ldquo ; Stop , &amp;rdquo ; Lorraine told him . &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s too late . Just take your daughter into the bedroom and shut the door . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Now 10 , Melony&amp;rsquo ; s daughter still doesn&amp;rsquo ; t know the circumstances of her mother&amp;rsquo ; s death . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Nearly three-and-a-half years after Melony died of a fentanyl overdose on her bathroom floor , not a day passes that Lorraine doesn&amp;rsquo ; t think of her daughter and that Saturday morning . She&amp;rsquo ; s coping , learning to live with the what-ifs and the might-have-beens . Would Melony be alive today if Lorraine had forced her way through the bathroom door an hour sooner ? It&amp;rsquo ; s a question she constantly asks herself . She doesn&amp;rsquo ; t have an answer . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;19729;TOOLONG ... title= " Lorraine Lefebvre poses for a photo in Ottawa Friday Nov 15 , 2019 . Lorraine lost her daughter , Melony , to an opioid overdose in 2016. " alt= " Lorraine Lefebvre poses for a photo in Ottawa Friday Nov 15 , 2019 . Lorraine lost her daughter , Melony , to an opioid overdose in 2016. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;19765;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509019 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2008 , 14 people in Ottawa died as a result of unintentional opioid overdoses . In 2016 , the year that Melony died , there were 40. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Last year , despite ongoing harm-reduction efforts aimed at preventing such deaths , that number ballooned to 82 , a nearly six-fold increase in the city&amp;rsquo ; s opioid mortality rate over the past decade , underlining the exploding emergency that communities , lawmakers , health-care professionals and families have so far been powerless to even contain , let alone eliminate . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And make no mistake : this is a crisis . Last year alone , nearly 4,600 Canadians died of apparent opioid-related causes , or one person every hour and 55 minutes . The current mortality rate from opioids in this country is about twice that of HIV/AIDS at its peak in 1995 , while opioid overdoses take as many lives in Canada every four days as the number that died in this country as a result of the SARS epidemic . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Opioids have claimed more Canadian lives than any infectious epidemic since the Spanish flu that ravaged the world following the First World War , while in Ottawa , opioids are closing even that gap : if current trends continue , the death toll from opioid overdoses since 2005 in Ottawa &amp;mdash ; it stood at 444 heading into 2019 &amp;mdash ; will , before the end of this year , eclipse the roughly 520 Ottawans who perished from the flu . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2015-16 , meanwhile , more than nine million opioid prescriptions were filled in Ontario &amp;mdash ; two for every three residents . In Ottawa , the rate was only slightly lower , with 57 prescriptions for every 100 people . Nearly two million Ontarians &amp;mdash ; 14 per cent of the population &amp;mdash ; filled opioid prescriptions that year , while an estimated one in eight people taking opioids for chronic pain develops an addiction , or substance use disorder . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But like a distant radio station fading in and out during a long car ride , the general public&amp;rsquo ; s awareness of the growing catastrophe comes in fits and starts , typically brought into focus when two or three deaths among suburban youths , compressed into just a few days , are reported . Hands are wrung , thoughts and prayers offered , calls to action made , and then things return to normal . The trouble with normal in this case , however , is that it&amp;rsquo ; s worsening at an alarming rate . Frontline responders are unable to keep up , using , as Ottawa Inner City Health executive director Wendy Muckle describes it , &amp;ldquo ; a teacup to put out a house fire . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The bodies , &amp;rdquo ; she warns , &amp;ldquo ; are piling up . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And they&amp;rsquo ; re piling up everywhere . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h3&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;19795;TOOLONG ... THE NUMBERS : A look at the opioid crisis in Ottawa and **27;19830;TOOLONG &lt;p&gt; Too long considered a problem confined to the alleyways and shelters of Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s marginalized inner-city street population , the opioid crisis has bled into workplaces , schools and living rooms throughout the city . There is no neighbourhood , no economic or social group , with an exemption . Public Health Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s Opioid Mortality Surveillance Report , issued in August , noted that in the 12-month period ending in June 2018 , almost three-quarters of the province&amp;rsquo ; s 1,209 accidental opioid-related deaths occurred in a private dwelling , and that more than half of those happened when the victim was alone . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s a real scourge , &amp;rdquo ; says Peter Eady , whose son , Simon , died in 2017 of an opioid overdose in the family&amp;rsquo ; s home near The Ottawa Hospital&amp;rsquo ; s Civic campus . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Peter was going out shopping one Saturday morning &amp;mdash ; Feb. 25 , 2017 &amp;mdash ; but first descended to the basement where Simon lay sleeping on a couch . It was about 9 a.m , and Peter took care not to wake the sprawling 24-year-old as he readjusted Simon&amp;rsquo ; s blanket . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;19859;TOOLONG ... title= " Simon Steinhoff , who died of an opioid overdose in his Civic Hospital-area home in 2017 . ( Photo : Provided by family of Simon Steinhoff ) " alt= " Simon Steinhoff , who died of an opioid overdose in his Civic Hospital-area home in 2017 . ( Photo : Provided by family of Simon Steinhoff ) " data-portal-copyright= " Courtesy of Peter Eady " **28;19895;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509083 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; The day before , the two had returned from Toronto on the train together . Peter , an account manager for a lighting company , was there on business , while Simon was visiting his girlfriend and other friends . Simon had recently lived in Toronto where he&amp;rsquo ; d attended George Brown University , but anxiety and depression made living on his own difficult , so he&amp;rsquo ; d returned the previous fall to Ottawa , where he could benefit from his parents&amp;rsquo ; support . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Simon took anti-anxiety medication and was getting help at the Somerset West Community Health Centre on Eccles Street . &amp;ldquo ; He was getting better by his own measure , &amp;rdquo ; recalls Peter . Simon had found part-time work doing repairs at an electronics business , and was applying to study engineering at Algonquin College . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; He said he was feeling better , &amp;rdquo ; Peter recalls , &amp;ldquo ; but obviously he was still searching for something , a solution to whatever pain or anxiety he was feeling , or blotting out whatever it was . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; After returning to Ottawa from Toronto , they had pizza for dinner and watched a movie . Peter and Simon&amp;rsquo ; s mother , Kathie Steinhoff , had been following the recent stories of opioid overdose-related deaths in Kanata &amp;mdash ; 14-year-old Chloe Kotval just 10 days earlier , and 18-year-old Teslin Russell weeks before that &amp;mdash ; and thought , &amp;lsquo ; Those poor kids &amp;hellip ; &amp;rsquo ; Steinhoff had even texted Simon that day to simply say , &amp;ldquo ; Thank you for not ODing on opioids. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It was a &amp;ldquo ; strange and stupid&amp;rdquo ; thing to write , she later said , &amp;ldquo ; and it haunts me . I suppose it was my clumsy way of raising a few issues . I had hoped to talk to my son about the poor teens , but I never got the chance . And now my life has been upended by this crisis . My heart broken . Like so many others . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;19925;TOOLONG ... title= " Peter Eady poses for a photo in Ottawa Friday Nov 15 , 2019 . Peter lost his son Simon to an opioid overdose. " alt= " Peter Eady poses for a photo in Ottawa Friday Nov 15 , 2019 . Peter lost his son Simon to an opioid overdose. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;19961;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509085 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; At around 11 a.m. that Saturday , Kathie took a coffee downstairs to Simon , and discovered him dead . Peter was only blocks away when his phone rang . &amp;ldquo ; Are you driving ? Can you come home ? &amp;rdquo ; Kathie asked . &amp;ldquo ; Simon&amp;rsquo ; s dead . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Simon&amp;rsquo ; s parents have no reason to believe their son was a regular opioid user . &amp;ldquo ; We think it was an experimental thing for him , &amp;rdquo ; says Peter . &amp;ldquo ; That&amp;rsquo ; s the impression we have from talking to his close friends . But you never know . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The manner of Simon&amp;rsquo ; s death not only points to a growing trend in opioid use in Canada , but is also illustrative of some of the difficulties faced by health-care providers grappling with the issue . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As Rob Boyd , director of the Oasis harm reduction program at the Sandy Hill Community Health Centre , points out , the homeless or at-risk population that accounts for roughly 10 per cent of accidental opioid deaths in Ontario was once the group hardest to pin down and treat . These days , they&amp;rsquo ; re the people most often taking part in harm reduction , such as attending supervised consumption sites . It&amp;rsquo ; s the construction workers who are self-medicating with illicit substances while alone in their basements , or the high school students using drugs of uncertain strengths , who often don&amp;rsquo ; t see themselves in this picture , he says , so precautions such as not consuming substances alone and having a naloxone kit handy &amp;mdash ; which can reverse the effects of an overdose &amp;mdash ; go unheeded . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For many , too , a substance use disorder , or addiction , attaches itself to an unbearable weight of stigma and shame . &amp;ldquo ; The message is getting out to call 911 in all overdoses , even if it is reversed with naloxone , because the overdose can return when the naloxone wears off , &amp;rdquo ; says Matthew Young , a senior research and policy analyst at the Canadian Centre for Substance Use and Addiction ( CCSA ) . &amp;ldquo ; But there&amp;rsquo ; s a lot of reluctance to do that because it&amp;rsquo ; s a stigmatized , illegal activity , and a lot of people who are reticent to call 911 are those who have experienced an overdose in the suburban environment , because they don&amp;rsquo ; t want the police showing up at their house , even if it&amp;rsquo ; s a loved one who is there . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Peter and Kathie are speaking out about Simon&amp;rsquo ; s death because they feel that not talking about it only serves to reinforce the shame aspect of overdose fatalities . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We&amp;rsquo ; re finally seeing people have open and honest discussions about the problem , &amp;rdquo ; says Peter . &amp;ldquo ; Would I have said this five or 10 years ago ? No , maybe I would have just hung my head in shame . But I&amp;rsquo ; m not ashamed of my son . I&amp;rsquo ; m sad , and it will never go away , but we don&amp;rsquo ; t shame people with cancer , we don&amp;rsquo ; t shame people with broken ribs . We&amp;rsquo ; re just getting around to not shaming people with mental illness . That&amp;rsquo ; s breaking the wall , and I think once that wall is a bit more broken down , we can get around to the substance use . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In Simon&amp;rsquo ; s case , &amp;rdquo ; he adds , &amp;ldquo ; he had anxiety and some depression , and so what do you do ? If you&amp;rsquo ; re not getting to the core of that , you&amp;rsquo ; re going to start self-medicating . Cocaine , alcohol , opioids , or a mix of all three . That&amp;rsquo ; s what people do . If you don&amp;rsquo ; t have good social and mental-health support , as well as realistic drug education , that&amp;rsquo ; s what will happen . Because of the shame and the lack of support , this is what people will do , and they will do it in the most dangerous way possible : late at night , in your basement , when no one&amp;rsquo ; s watching , when you&amp;rsquo ; ve had a couple of drinks . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;19991;TOOLONG ... title= " Simon Eady ( R ) with his sister , Samantha . Simon was 24 when he died of an opioid overdose in 2017. " alt= " Simon Eady ( R ) with his sister , Samantha . Simon was 24 when he died of an opioid overdose in 2017. " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;20027;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509084 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Throw in a drug supply that includes counterfeit pills or other drugs laced with fentanyl , a synthetic opioid up to 100 times more potent than morphine , or carfentanil , a veterinary opioid 10,000 times as potent as morphine , and the results , as we&amp;rsquo ; ve seen , are disastrous . People are often overdosing on opioids while completely oblivious that they&amp;rsquo ; re even taking them , or believing they&amp;rsquo ; re consuming a prescription opioid &amp;mdash ; Percocet , for example &amp;mdash ; unaware that what they&amp;rsquo ; re taking is an illegally pressed pill possibly containing a lethal dose . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; You don&amp;rsquo ; t know whether it&amp;rsquo ; s a street drug or a pharmaceutical drug or whatever , &amp;rdquo ; says Peter . &amp;ldquo ; You really don&amp;rsquo ; t know what&amp;rsquo ; s in there . The average person taking it isn&amp;rsquo ; t a pharmacist , isn&amp;rsquo ; t a chemist , and doesn&amp;rsquo ; t understand what might happen . And somebody might be able to wake up the next morning and feel a bit dazed , and other people might not wake up . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , people with substance use disorders who don&amp;rsquo ; t die from their overdoses are increasingly turning up at hospital emergency departments . In 2018 , Ottawa-area hospitals reported 471 emergency department visits for opioid overdoses , a 27 per cent increase over the 370 a year earlier , and almost twice the 243 seen in 2016. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Current measures in place , including supervised consumption sites ( Ottawa has four : Ottawa Inner City Health&amp;rsquo ; s trailer at the Shepherds of Good Hope on Murray Street ; the Sandy Hill Community Health Centre , on Nelson Street ; Somerset West Community Health Centre , on Eccles Street ; and Ottawa Public Health&amp;rsquo ; s , on Clarence Street ) ; legislated changes to Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s pharmaceutical drug supply ; widespread access to naloxone ; and agonist treatment &amp;mdash ; replacing such drugs as heroin and fentanyl with safer , longer-lasting opioids such as methadone or suboxone , to ease withdrawal symptoms &amp;mdash ; are only serving to slow the invasive tide , not reverse it , as the number of deaths continues to rise each year . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;20057;TOOLONG ... title= " Rob Boyd , Director of Oasis Program poses for a photo at the Sandy Hill Community Health Centre in Ottawa Thursday April 25 , 2019 . The health centre wanted to show how they use a mass spectrometer to determine whether or not a product has any carfentanyl. " alt= " Rob Boyd , Director of Oasis Program poses for a photo at the Sandy Hill Community Health Centre in Ottawa Thursday April 25 , 2019 . The health centre wanted to show how they use a mass spectrometer to determine whether or not a product has any carfentanyl. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;20093;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509077 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; At Sandy Hill&amp;rsquo ; s supervised consumption site , people who use substances can test them to find out what they contain . It&amp;rsquo ; s the only such program in Ottawa , while Vancouver is the only other Canadian city currently offering the service . At Sandy Hill , they&amp;rsquo ; ve discovered fentanyl in everything : heroin , cocaine , crack , even cannabis . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We see swings of the different drugs on a regular basis , &amp;rdquo ; says Ottawa Inner City Health medical director Dr. Jeffrey Turnbull . &amp;ldquo ; We always have fentanyl now . The one thing that is not in the heroin &amp;hellip ; is heroin . It&amp;rsquo ; s almost all fentanyl or carfentanil or some other drug &amp;ndash ; benzodiazepines , methadone &amp;mdash ; mixed in with it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There are newer metabolites available all the time , &amp;rdquo ; he adds . &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s small amounts , easy to traffic , can be synthesized relatively easily , and very addictive and very potent . And it&amp;rsquo ; s very hard to detect . And you&amp;rsquo ; ve got a ready market for it . Wouldn&amp;rsquo ; t this be your dream if you were in the drug trade ? So I think we&amp;rsquo ; re going to have a lot of trouble arresting our way out of this problem . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; According to Statistics Canada , the opioid crisis is the main factor behind its findings earlier this year that the life expectancy of Canadians at birth failed to increase from 2016 to 2017 , as deaths due to opioids wiped out health gains made by improved treatments for cancer and heart disease . It marked the first time in four decades that Canadians&amp;rsquo ; average life expectancy stalled . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; How did we get here ? Both Boyd and Turnbull refer to the &amp;ldquo ; perfect storm&amp;rdquo ; of events that laid this mess at our feet , beginning in the 1980s when palliative care and pain management became a hot-button issue in Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Turnbull , who was then a practising physician , says that pain was not being adequately addressed at the time , and many among the palliative-care movement were arguing that , like blood pressure , pulse and respiration , it should be treated as a vital sign . Some physicians , however , voiced their concerns about patients possibly becoming addicted to painkillers. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Then along came the Purdue Pharma&amp;rsquo ; s 1996 release of OxyContin , a powerful oxycodone painkiller that was added to Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s drug formulary &amp;mdash ; meaning doctors here could prescribe it &amp;mdash ; in 2000 . The drug was effective and , Purdue promised , not addictive . &amp;ldquo ; And we just jumped on it , &amp;rdquo ; recalls Turnbull . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;20123;TOOLONG ... title= " FILE PHOTO : Bottles of prescription painkiller OxyContin , 40mg pills , made by Purdue Pharma L.D. sit on a shelf at a local pharmacy , in Provo , Utah , U.S. , April 25 , 2017. " alt= " FILE PHOTO : Bottles of prescription painkiller OxyContin , 40mg pills , made by Purdue Pharma L.D. sit on a shelf at a local pharmacy , in Provo , Utah , U.S. , April 25 , 2017. " data-portal-copyright= " George Frey " **28;20159;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509082 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; As it turned out , though , the drug was highly addictive . Many people were bypassing its slow-release formulation by crushing the pills and snorting the powder , or mixing it with water and injecting it . Street users , says Turnbull , called it hillbilly heroin . &amp;ldquo ; It was being widely used . But those messages were not being heard . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; And then ( Purdue ) lost their patent protection . You would think that there was an opportunity to say , &amp;lsquo ; No , we&amp;rsquo ; re not going to open this up to the world , &amp;rsquo ; but instead , we opened it up to the world , and prescriptions went up . &amp;rdquo ; In 2010 , meanwhile , as a way of extending its hold on the market , Purdue , citing greater safety precautions , released a new formulation for OxyContin , called OxyNEO , that was more difficult to crush or inject . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As Oxy&amp;rsquo ; s addictive properties had become more evident , doctors were increasingly reluctant to prescribe it , and in 2012 , OxyContin was delisted from Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s formularly . OxyNEO was added , but its availability was more restricted . This , says Turnbull , completed the first phase of the perfect storm : create a substance addiction epidemic &amp;mdash ; an estimated 300,000 to 400,000 Ontarians had by then become addicted to opioids , he says &amp;mdash ; and then remove the substance . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The second phase of the storm was the illicit drug trade that filled the newly created vacuum . &amp;ldquo ; We didn&amp;rsquo ; t have treatment facilities or counselling , &amp;rdquo ; says Turnbull , &amp;ldquo ; and who was there waiting for that group of people but the people who produce heroin ? &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Suddenly , the banged-up thirtysome-year-old construction worker &amp;mdash ; the industry identified as perhaps the hardest-hit by the opioid crisis , accounting for about 30 per cent of accidental fatalities in Ontario last year where the deceased&amp;rsquo ; s occupation was known &amp;mdash ; was associating with criminal elements to address their pain , and by and large using the substance alone in their home . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Then came the fentanyl wave . The drug , up to 100 times more potent than morphine , had been around in patch form , typically used by cancer patients , since the 1990s . But it wasn&amp;rsquo ; t until the 2012 delisting of OxyContin that it found greater widespread use . By 2015 or &amp;rsquo ; 16 , says Turnbull , it had become a recreational drug . Health-care and enforcement officials in Ottawa helplessly watched as it spread west , from B.C. to Alberta and , eventually , Ontario . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We knew it was going to be Phase 3 , &amp;rdquo ; says Turnbull . &amp;ldquo ; We had all these people on heroin and we had a highly addictive new drug coming , and it was easy to synthesize and easy to transport . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It just happened overnight , and it devastated the community . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;20189;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Jeff Turnbull of Ottawa Inner City Health . " alt= " Dr. Jeff Turnbull of Ottawa Inner City Health . " data-portal-copyright= " Jean Levac " **28;20225;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509066 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; According to Boyd , the switch from largely stolen or otherwise diverted fentanyl patches to illicitly manufactured powdered fentanyl was a tipping point . &amp;ldquo ; Nobody saw it coming , and it was such a game-changer . It changed all the rules to how we were approaching this problem and how we were going to solve it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Fentanyl was going to create challenges in and of itself , &amp;rdquo ; he adds , &amp;ldquo ; but once we got to the illicit fentanyl , we really lost control . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Globalization , advances in information technology and the dark web also combined to make matters worse , says Matthew Young from the CCSA . Many of the illicit synthetic opioids that came on the market were based on analgesics that had been tested by pharmaceutical companies but never commercially developed . It wasn&amp;rsquo ; t that difficult for a trafficker to Google an old patent and send the specifications to a chemical manufacturer in China to have it produced . The dealer would then mix the chemical with powder , stamp it out as pills , and start selling it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Young notes that the Drug Enforcement Agency in the U.S. and the RCMP here in Canada have done a lot of work with their colleagues in China to curb this practice . Five or six years ago when it was more prevalent , part of the attraction to dealers was that the drugs had never actually been on the pharmaceutical market before , and because they weren&amp;rsquo ; t specifically referred to in Canada&amp;rsquo ; s Controlled Drugs and Substances Act , they technically weren&amp;rsquo ; t illegal . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; But there&amp;rsquo ; s a saying in toxicology that the dose makes the poison , &amp;rdquo ; says Young , &amp;ldquo ; and the challenge with a potent chemical like fentanyl and carfentanil is that these clandestine labs had a very challenging time getting an even distribution of the substance throughout the product that they were going to sell . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In other words , while one pill or injection might get you to that sweet spot you&amp;rsquo ; d been chasing , the next one you take an hour later could well kill you . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; So while it was bad enough to have thousands and thousands of people addicted to opioids , the crisis became exponentially worse when increasing numbers of them started showing up in morgues . And as long as illicit fentanyl and more toxic analogues such as carfentanil are contaminating the street drug supply , the problem , says Boyd , isn&amp;rsquo ; t going to improve . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Fundamentally , we really have to address this toxic supply of drugs that is out there . We have to go back to the problem we had in 2012 , which was the problem with OxyContin . We need to go back to a point where there was at least some level of control . Fentanyl has really challenged all the tools that we have to fight this . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Adding to the perfect storm described by Turnbull , Boyd points to other factors that exacerbated the opioid crisis &amp;mdash ; an aging population that feels entitled to live pain-free ; a strained primary health-care system focused on the flow-through of patients , making the prescription of drugs preferred over other , longer-term , treatments ; and a shift to a knowledge-based economy that pushed great swaths of the labour force to under- or unemployment , as jobs moved offshore to Third World countries . It&amp;rsquo ; s no coincidence that the opioid crisis is particularly bad in what are considered rust-belt communities , where auto plants have shuttered and other manufacturing industries closed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;20255;TOOLONG ... title= " A Drug Enforcement Administration ( DEA ) chemist checks confiscated powder containing fentanyl at the DEA Northeast Regional Laboratory on October 8 , 2019 in New York . " alt= " A Drug Enforcement Administration ( DEA ) chemist checks confiscated powder containing fentanyl at the DEA Northeast Regional Laboratory on October 8 , 2019 in New York . " data-portal-copyright= " DON EMMERT " **28;20291;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509071 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Think of London , Ontario when ( General Motors ) was pulling out of there , and Oshawa now , &amp;rdquo ; says Boyd . In the U.S. , it&amp;rsquo ; s West Virginia , Ohio , Pennsylvania and Kentucky that are hurting the most . &amp;ldquo ; Those are the areas of highest prescribing of opioids because people didn&amp;rsquo ; t have anything to do , &amp;rdquo ; says Boyd . &amp;ldquo ; You can put up with a lot of pain if you&amp;rsquo ; ve got a job to get up and go to and if you have responsibilities . But you take that away and the depression and pain come in &amp;mdash ; mood disorders and depression can manifest as pain in your body . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; So people are feeling unwell and unhappy . They&amp;rsquo ; re depressed and are legitimately feeling aches and pains . So you&amp;rsquo ; ve got downturns in the economy and social disruption happens from that . There&amp;rsquo ; s a growing sense of disconnect . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That growing sense of disconnect is a major theme in journalist/researcher Johann Hari&amp;rsquo ; s book Chasing the Scream : The First and Last Days of the War on Drugs . &amp;ldquo ; The opposite of addiction isn&amp;rsquo ; t sobriety , &amp;rdquo ; he insists , &amp;ldquo ; it&amp;rsquo ; s connection . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s position as a white-collar , government and hi-tech city has given it some insulation from the towering mortality rates that have plagued many other Ontario municipalities. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The city&amp;rsquo ; s 82 accidental opioid-related deaths last year put its rate at 8.1 deaths for every 100,000 residents , slightly less than the provincial average of 10.2 and well below Thunder Bay&amp;rsquo ; s 28.5 , the highest in Ontario . ( Were Ottawa to match Thunder Bay&amp;rsquo ; s rate , the death toll last year would have been 288 , or 11 people every two weeks . ) &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Other cities or regions with extremely high mortality rates last year included Hamilton ( 21.3 per 100,000 ) , Niagara ( 19.2 ) , Peterborough ( 18.8 ) , Haliburton ( 16.2 ) and Sudbury ( 16.0 ) . Toronto&amp;rsquo ; s 2018 rate of 10 people per 100,000 was just under the provincial average , while London , Windsor , Kingston , North Bay and Kitchener/Waterloo were all slightly higher . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There are a lot of protective factors for people around substance use and substance use disorder , &amp;rdquo ; says Boyd . &amp;ldquo ; Education , stable housing , basic economic stability , and that other piece around social connections . And a lot of that comes through employment , so if you&amp;rsquo ; ve got good employment , you&amp;rsquo ; re probably OK . So Ottawa is a bit of a bubble , with the public service . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;20321;TOOLONG ... title= " Wendy Muckle of Ottawa Inner City Health . " alt= " Wendy Muckle of Ottawa Inner City Health . " data-portal-copyright= " Jean Levac " **28;20357;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509068 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ottawa , says Muckle , somewhat benefits from its size : it&amp;rsquo ; s large enough of a city that it has numerous agencies and services that might not be available to residents of smaller communities , while at the same time not being so large as to have to duplicate services . &amp;ldquo ; Ottawa tends to be able to have a more coordinated approach , &amp;rdquo ; she says . &amp;ldquo ; Everyone tends to do things together , whereas in Toronto there tends to be a group working on this and another group working on that , because it&amp;rsquo ; s so big . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s opioid mortality is nonetheless exploding , in both number and rate , as well as against the provincial average : In 2012 , Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s opioid-related mortality rate was 2.1 per 100,000 , less than half of the province&amp;rsquo ; s 4.4 that year . Last year&amp;rsquo ; s rate of 8.1 &amp;mdash ; nearly quadruple the 2012 rate &amp;mdash ; was about 80 per cent of the Ontario rate . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Other indicators add to a less-than-rosy picture here : the 444 opioid-related visits to emergency rooms in Ottawa last year was 20 per cent more than in 2017 , and an increase of nearly 150 per cent from the 178 trips in 2015. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;bull ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Many at the front lines believe that the opioid crisis has to be treated as a health crisis , and not a criminal one . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If this was something else , like cancer , we&amp;rsquo ; d be taking it far more seriously , &amp;rdquo ; says Peter Eady . &amp;ldquo ; But because there&amp;rsquo ; s that base level of puritanism in our society , we treat it like , &amp;lsquo ; Just say no , &amp;rsquo ; and we know that&amp;rsquo ; s not effective . The prohibition model hasn&amp;rsquo ; t worked . The war on drugs was lost years ago , and all it&amp;rsquo ; s done is destroy countries like Bolivia , Columbia , Mexico and the , let&amp;rsquo ; s call them the consumer societies &amp;mdash ; U.S. , Western Europe and whatnot. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He cites Portugal and Switzerland as examples of countries where progressive policies , including decriminalization and substitution therapy , have had considerable success easing the problem . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He additionally praises such initiatives as Project Step , an Ottawa-based organization that provides support , treatment , education and prevention of youth addictions , as well as the Community Addictions Peer Support Association and The Canadian Drug Policy Coalition . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; But we could do a lot better , &amp;rdquo ; he says , &amp;ldquo ; and the present methods ( of criminalization and incarceration ) aren&amp;rsquo ; t working . Even most thoughtful law-enforcement officials know that . If we had a safe supply and better integrated it with the health system , I think you&amp;rsquo ; d get a much better result . The stats around the world seem to suggest it . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; At least it&amp;rsquo ; s now being discussed openly , &amp;rdquo ; he says , &amp;ldquo ; and maybe things will change . I can only hope so . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;20387;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Vera Etches , head of Ottawa Public Health , in Ottawa Friday Oct 25 , 2019. " alt= " Dr. Vera Etches , head of Ottawa Public Health , in Ottawa Friday Oct 25 , 2019. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;20423;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509067 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Vera Etches , Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s chief medical officer of health , notes that there are about 40,000 people in Ottawa who have said they used illicit substances in the last year . &amp;ldquo ; Not everyone is in trouble with that use ; there&amp;rsquo ; s a spectrum of use . Some people find beneficial aspects &amp;mdash ; they&amp;rsquo ; re stable and able to manage their lives and their families . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; But when someone starts to have a disorder , that&amp;rsquo ; s a crisis for each of those families . When you see 82 deaths and you see the deaths rising , that&amp;rsquo ; s distressing . And each of those families and many more are in crisis . So to me , the label you put on it is not as important as the action we&amp;rsquo ; re taking to address it . What&amp;rsquo ; s important is , are we moving ahead urgently ? Because people are in distress and things are going the wrong way still . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At its budget presentation to the Ottawa Board of Health earlier this month , Ottawa Public Health recommended the adoption of the Ottawa Community Action Plan , a mental health and substance use strategy focusing on opioid use . The plan proposes collaborative efforts by OPH , the CCSA , The Royal , the Community Addictions Peer Support Association and other stakeholders to address stigma and language issues related to substance use and mental health ; an increase in harm reduction initiatives and access to them ; and integrating access to comprehensive mental health , substance use and social services . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That said , Ottawa Public Health also had to find $1.4 million in &amp;ldquo ; efficiencies&amp;rdquo ; in its 2020 budget . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Can the crisis deepen ? &amp;ldquo ; Yes , &amp;rdquo ; says Turnbull , &amp;ldquo ; it can get a whole lot worse , with new derivatives that are more toxic , or if it gets out and spreads beyond the traditional drug-using population and gets in high schools and other areas where it becomes more recreational . Absolutely it can get worse . At the moment , we&amp;rsquo ; re struggling to keep up and to keep people alive . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Etches agrees , and wonders , too , how social determinants could further come into play . &amp;ldquo ; What if unemployment increases ? What if the rates of poverty increase ? Those to me would be big drivers of stress , and then of potential substance use , and then problematic substance use . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We want to be building a society where there are supports when people need them to keep them out of dire situations . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Other agencies are engaging the crisis . The Ottawa chapter of Bereaved Families of Ontario , for example , which offers peer support groups at the Bronson Centre for people grieving the loss of a family member or friend , this month started a group dedicated to those who have lost someone to substance use . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;20453;TOOLONG ... title= " Lorraine Lefebvre daughter , Melony as a youngster . Melony died of an opioid overdose in 2016 , at the age of 42. " alt= " Lorraine Lefebvre daughter , Melony as a youngster . Melony died of an opioid overdose in 2016 , at the age of 42. " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;20489;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509079 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Lorraine Lefebvre , who was in therapy following her daughter Melony&amp;rsquo ; s death , had heard two years ago of Bereaved Families&amp;rsquo ; 10-week support group for those who had lost a child , so she joined . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It helped me immensely , to deal with a lot of the anger , the guilt , the shame and grief , &amp;rdquo ; she says . &amp;ldquo ; It doesn&amp;rsquo ; t go away , but I&amp;rsquo ; ve learned to live with it . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; This past May , Lorraine started volunteering in BFO&amp;rsquo ; s office , keeping records , setting up chairs for meetings , getting the coffee and cookies ready , greeting people at meetings and signing them in. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I found my purpose , &amp;rdquo ; she says . &amp;ldquo ; It gave me joy to come in here every day . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; More recently , Lorraine completed the facilitators&amp;rsquo ; course , and is now leading the loss-of-child group . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She says that since she began volunteering there six months ago , 56 new clients have come to the centre , each having lost someone to substance use . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Why is no one paying attention ? &amp;rdquo ; she asks . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **30;20519;TOOLONG " **37;20551;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;20590;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " http : **34;20632;TOOLONG ... they buy those drugs , it 's a loaded gun ' &lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;20668;TOOLONG ... no blood test for addiction ' : Troubling data in the opioid **27;20703;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;20732;TOOLONG ... shame of opioids : Confronting the crisis by addressing **27;20767;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;20796;TOOLONG ... Ottawa take notes from other countries dealing with an opioid crisis ? &lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;20831;TOOLONG ... off fentanyl : ' It takes all your cares and puts them aside ' &lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;/ul&gt; Bruce DeachmanSat , 23 Nov 2019 21:31:18 **45;20866;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal NewsMenWomenProposed Ontario drug regulation changes worry policy expertshttp : **33;20913;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;20948;TOOLONG ... title= " A 2016 file photo of Dr. Joel Lexchin. " alt= " A 2016 file photo of Dr. Joel Lexchin. " data-portal-copyright= " Tyler Anderson/Naitonal Post " **28;20984;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1509861 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ontario government is proposing drug regulation changes that it says will remove red tape and barriers for pharmaceutical companies , allowing some drugs to bypass the normal approval process during a shortage . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21014;TOOLONG ... shortages , including the often-used breast cancer drug tamoxifen&lt;/a&gt; , have been wide-spread and increasing in recent years . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The move , the government says , would allow alternative drug products to quickly enter the market during a shortage . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; However , the move is being met with resistance by some drug policy experts who say more information is needed to ensure the changes are good for consumers &amp;mdash ; and whether the changes will actually help prevent long-term drug shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The changes , part of the government&amp;rsquo ; s larger push to reduce red tape , would also make it easier for companies to raise or lower drug prices in other cases , depending on the market . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In a document about the proposed changes , the government says they would ensure that Ontario remains &amp;ldquo ; open for business&amp;rdquo ; for the pharmacy and pharmaceutical industry . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The proposals , called &amp;ldquo ; Reducing Administrative Burden to Drug Manufacturers and Pharmacies , &amp;rdquo ; are part of an omnibus red tape reduction bill that was introduced at the end of October while the legislature was recessed . They affect prescription drugs , mainly generics. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some drug policy experts fear the changes would remove some consumer protections , are being introduced with little publicity and will do little to address long-term drug shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Pharmaceutical policy expert Dr. Joel Lexchin , who teaches at York University&amp;rsquo ; s School of Health Policy and Management , said he is concerned about the lack of consultation and transparency around the changes . Some of the proposals respond to requests from the pharmaceutical industry with apparently little or no input from consumers or healthcare providers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; More details are needed , he said , before the changes go ahead . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It should not be the job of the Ontario Ministry of Health and Long-Term Care to advocate on behalf of the pharmaceutical industry . The job of the Ministry of Health should be the protection of the health of Ontarians , &amp;rdquo ; wrote Lexchin in a submission to the Ministry of Health . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Before the ministry goes ahead with these changes it needs to provide much more information and show that these changes will put the health of the people of Ontario above the interests of the commercial entities that are advocating for them . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Jacalyn Duffin , professor emerita and Hannah Chair of the History of Medicine at Queen&amp;rsquo ; s University , said she only learned about the proposals when a law firm blogged about them . She said she finds some of them shocking . &amp;ldquo ; They will remove some consumer protections and probably will not help the shortages . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The proposed changes include an amendment that would make it easier for generic drug companies to adjust their prices if the number of competitors on the market changes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Under the current model , generic drugs are priced at a percentage of the brand price , depending on how many competitors there are . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For example , if a brand label drug costs $10 , generic makers could charge $7.50 or $8 , but that price would decrease based on the number of competitors . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The proposed changes allow drug companies to more easily adjust their prices when competitors come in and out of the market , something the government says should reduce the price of generic drugs . However , it could also allow companies to increase the price of generics , especially at a time of consolidations of drug companies and shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Justin Bates , Chief Executive Officer of the Ontario Pharmacists Association , said the change enables Ontario pharmacies to operate on a level playing field with pharmacies in every other province , except Quebec . Ordinary commercial terms are a normal part of business , he said , and do not increase drug costs , which are regulated by a Canada-wide system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The proposed changes would also exempt certain drugs from drug submission requirements , allowing quicker funding of a drug that could fill in during a shortage .
