Funding of the alternative drug would be temporary , the government says . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A spokesman for Health Minister Christine Elliott dismissed concerns that the changes had been brought in quietly and with too little information , saying they were part of a &amp;ldquo ; high-profile&amp;rdquo ; government wide announcement of red tape reduction proposals and will help the government respond to patient needs in the event of potential drug shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The proposed changes would allow an alternative drug product to be temporarily funded under the Ontario Drug Benefit program during a supply interruption that may be due to a drug shortage &amp;hellip ; and is expected to lead to significant patient risk or harm , &amp;rdquo ; said Hayley Chazan , a spokesperson for Health Minister Christine Elliott . &amp;ldquo ; This proposal , if approved , would mitigate supply gaps of funded products for recipients of the Ontario Drug Benefit program . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But Lexchin said the proposed changes are simply reactive and lack any proactive measures to deal with future drug shortages . New Zealand , for example , requires drug companies to guarantee there will be sufficient supply before approving the drug for the health system . If the company runs out of drugs , it is then their responsibility to source a replacement , not the government&amp;rsquo ; s. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Paul Grootendorst , associate professor in the Faculty of Pharmacy at the University of Toronto said the proposed changes allowing generic companies to change their prices up or down when the number of competitors changes make sense . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I don&amp;rsquo ; t see any problems , &amp;rdquo ; he said of the proposals . &amp;ldquo ; These are more housekeeping issues . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The government is seeking comment on the proposals until Nov. 27. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; epayne@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21049;TOOLONG ... bodies are piling up ' : Inside the opioid epidemic and the crisis that wo n't go away&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21084;TOOLONG ... concerned they ca n't provide proper care in crowded hospitals , says union&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21119;TOOLONG ... was bawling ' : Injured Bronco 's mother stunned by his progress after surgery&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneFri , 22 Nov 2019 11:39:55 **45;21154;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal NewsSeniorsWomenU.S. stem cell clinic offering unapproved therapies brings direct-to-consumer marketing to Ottawahttp : **34;21201;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;21237;TOOLONG ... title= " A doctor a private U.S. clinic injects a patient with stem cells to treat cartilage wastage joint injury and pain . " alt= " A doctor a private U.S. clinic injects a patient with stem cells to treat cartilage wastage joint injury and pain . " data-portal-copyright= " edward olive " **28;21273;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1507409 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Just a few months after Health Canada began cracking down on private clinics offering unapproved stem cell therapies , at least one U.S. clinic has moved in to fill the vacuum with direct marketing to Canadian consumers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The clinic from Burlington , Vermont , even offers shuttle buses to transport people from Ottawa to the clinic four hours away for treatment it suggests will end joint pain , among other things . Lunch and dinner are free , but each injection costs $6,880 . Two for $10,880. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The treatments , using umbilical cord-derived mesenchymal stem cells , are not approved in either Canada or the United States . Health Canada warns that Canadians who travel abroad for stem cell treatments &amp;ldquo ; may put themselves at risk . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While stem cells , which were discovered at the University of Toronto in 1961 by James Till and Ernest McCulloch , promise to revolutionize many treatments and could offer breakthroughs for diseases , almost all are still considered experimental and have yet to be proven safe or effective . Clinical trials on numerous potential stem cell therapies are under way , including in Ottawa . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While research progresses , private stem cell clinics have popped up around the world making promises for treatments not yet proven safe or effective . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A 2018 study by Leigh Turner of the University of Minnesota Center for Bioethics found 43 clinics offering stem cell treatments in Canada and 750 in the U.S. Earlier this year , Health Canada sent Canadian clinics , including some in Ottawa , cease-and-desist letters . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Clinics in Vermont , near the Canadian border , appear to have ramped up marketing to Canadians since then . One clinic has been holding back-to-back seminars . Another says it stopped marketing in Canada after receiving a warning from Health Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There have been cases of harm as a result of treatments , including two women who had permanent damage to their sight after stem cells were injected into their eyes at a Florida clinic . Other patients have been infected with unsterilized equipment and others have developed tumours at the site of stem cell injections. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A common harm , critics say , is exploitation . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;21303;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Michael Rudnicki is director of the regenerative medicine program and Sprott Centre for Stem Cell Research at the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute , says of stem cell therapy claims : ' If it sounds too good to be true , it probably is too good to be true . ' " alt= " Dr. Michael Rudnicki is director of the regenerative medicine program and Sprott Centre for Stem Cell Research at the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute , says of stem cell therapy claims : ' If it sounds too good to be true , it probably is too good to be true . ' " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;21339;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1507406 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Health officials say the clinics are misusing the promise of stem cell therapy to exploit vulnerable patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; These patients are in pain and they are suffering and they are looking for help and they are being exploited , &amp;rdquo ; said Dr. Michael Rudnicki , director of the regenerative medicine program and Sprott Centre for Stem Cell Research at the Ottawa Hospital Research Institute . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If it sounds too good to be true , it probably is too good to be true . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At a recent seminar at a west-end Ottawa hotel meeting room , Roseanna Ammendolea of the Vermont Center for Regenerative Medicine told a packed room that her clinic and others like it had successfully treated people for pain related to arthritis , neuropathy and other ailments that affected joints using mesenchymal stem cells from umbilical cords . The stem cells , she claimed , are both effective and safe , saying there had been no issues with cell rejection . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We will not give injections if we feel that this injection will not be beneficial to our patients . This is why we are so successful . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Participants , including some who walked with canes and others who talked about being in pain and having mobility issues , were shown videos of people described as Canadian clients who claimed the treatments worked . One man said it was &amp;ldquo ; probably the best money I have spent in my life as far as my health . &amp;rdquo ; Another said she would do it again &amp;ldquo ; in a heartbeat&amp;rdquo ; and was able to do things she hadn&amp;rsquo ; t been able to do earlier . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; They were also shown a slide showing long wait times for hip and knee replacements in Ontario , &amp;ldquo ; We are not a priority , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &amp;ldquo ; Where does that leave us ? &amp;rdquo ; Participants weren&amp;rsquo ; t told exactly how the stem cells were supposed to work , but claimed they had successfully improved pain and mobility issues in clients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; What the seminar goers weren&amp;rsquo ; t told is that , even in the U.S. , the treatment is not covered by health insurance because it remains unproven. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The U.S. Federal Drug Administration has issued a warning to consumers not to use cell therapies that are unapproved or unproven. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Stem cells have been called everything from cure-alls to miracle treatments . But don&amp;rsquo ; t believe the hype . Some unscrupulous providers offer stem cell products that are both unapproved and unproven . So beware of potentially dangerous procedures &amp;mdash ; and confirm what&amp;rsquo ; s really being offered before you consider &lt;em&gt;any&lt;/em&gt; &amp;nbsp ; treatment , &amp;rdquo ; the FDA said in a statement . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The only stem-cell-based products that are FDA-approved for use in the United States are blood-forming stem cells derived from cord blood&amp;nbsp ; for limited use in patients with disorders affecting the body system that is involved in the production of blood . Bone marrow is also used for these treatments , but is generally not regulated by the FDA for that use . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Health Canada has granted market authorization for a stem cell therapy to treat graft-versus-host disease and two cell-based gene therapies to treat certain cancers . Most cell therapies are still experimental . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I totally understand the skepticism of it , &amp;rdquo ; Doug Argento , who works at the Vermont Center for Regenerative Medicine , said in a telephone interview , &amp;ldquo ; but the fact is that things that are approved now and medically paid for were seen as renegade 20 or 30 years ago . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The treatment employs technology developed by Neil Riordan , founder , chairman and chief science officer of the Stem Cell Institute in Panama , using human umbilical cord tissue-derived mesenchymal stem cells . There are 41 such clinics across the U.S. Riordan also played a role in the development of a nutritional product called Stem-Kine , which producers claim &amp;mdash ; without scientific backing &amp;mdash ; increases the number of stem cells circulating in a person&amp;rsquo ; s body . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The stem cells injected in the clinic , Argento said , are from umbilical cord tissue as a result of caesarean births to reduce risk of infection . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Rudnicki , of The Ottawa Hospital Research Institute , says there is &amp;ldquo ; no evidence that these sorts of cells are regenerative at all . It would not pass muster in Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The public has to understand that there are people out to remove them from their money . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Rudnicki says he regularly receives inquiries from people desperate to get stem cell treatments . He says he tries to connect them with clinical trials that they might be able to participate in. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Rudnicki noted there were multiple clinical trials in Canada , including treatments of autoimmune diseases , trials involving treatment for Type 1 diabetes and others . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; But the use of these inappropriate cell types for treating arthritis and joints and so on is certainly not approved by Health Canada and would not be allowed in Canada under the regulations . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There is some evidence that injections of some stem cell products might have a temporary positive impact on inflammation , he said , but it will not be regenerative and will not restore function to joints . &amp;ldquo ; They are being sold a bill of goods . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Leigh Turner of the University of Minnesota Center for Bioethics , meanwhile , says the explosion in clinics offering unproven stem cell therapies in the U.S. is &amp;ldquo ; a marketplace that traffics in misrepresentation . It is easy to see how people are taken advantage of and scammed. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It is also difficult to find out about physical harms being done to patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There are no safety studies . We don&amp;rsquo ; t have good data . But we do know there have been some serious harms. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Stem cell therapies have the potential to become standard treatment in some areas , but they are not there yet , Turner said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Businesses are tapping into genuine human suffering , desperation and also hope . &amp;ldquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Turner also noted there was an &amp;ldquo ; excellent chance&amp;rdquo ; that the vials of liquid being injected into patients did not actually contain stem cells . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Jonathan Fenton of another stem cell clinic in Burlington , the Vermont Regenerative Medicine , said he had complained about the new clinic , the Vermont Center for Regenerative Medicine , which has a similar name and employs hard-sell tactics , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; His clinic takes bone marrow from patients&amp;rsquo ; hips and injects it . The procedure is done the same day . He says he regularly sees Canadian patients for bone marrow aspiration therapy and platelet-rich plasma treatments , using their own blood . The treatments , he says , speed healing and are allowed in the U.S. The use of bone marrow aspiration is neither proven nor allowed in Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Fenton , who is secretary-treasurer of the American Academy of Orthopedic Medicine , acknowledged many people offering stem cell treatments are &amp;ldquo ; not doing it to the highest ethical standards . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He has filed complaints with state officials over clinics selling unsafe or fraudulent treatments . &amp;ldquo ; I have asked the state and federal judiciary to close down this clinic for committing fraud . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He said his platelet and bone marrow treatments were covered by a major Vermont health insurer because they saw the cost of benefits were going down and patients were requiring fewer surgeries . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He said he was told by Health Canada that he could not market in Canada . Representatives of the Vermont Center for Regenerative Medicine , meanwhile , said they had discussions with Health Canada about what they could and could not say when marketing in Canada before holding seminars in Ottawa and Halifax . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We have looked at the information provided and have not identified any immediate non-compliance with advertising regulations pertaining to Canadian health products , &amp;rdquo ; a Health Canada spokesperson said , adding that the agency was continuing to assess . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Back at a west-end Ottawa hotel , some participants in the seminar , including a retired pharmacist , said they were considering getting the treatments . &amp;ldquo ; But it&amp;rsquo ; s expensive . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Another participant said he was skeptical. &amp;ldquo ; They seemed very sketchy when I went online . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **27;21369;TOOLONG " **34;21398;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21434;TOOLONG ... and Kaushic : We 're losing the fight against superbugs&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21469;TOOLONG ... who live to at least 110 , boast stronger immune systems , helping them fight off diseases&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21504;TOOLONG ... stimulator ' really promising ' : Survivor moves legs for first time since Humboldt bus crash&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneMon , 18 Nov 2019 11:51:22 **45;21539;TOOLONG ... **37;21586;TOOLONG upsetting ' : Breast cancer patients dealing with shortage of common drug treatmenthttp : **31;21625;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;21658;TOOLONG ... title= " Tricia Smith poses for a photo in Ottawa Monday Nov 11 , 2019 . " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;21694;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1503625 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; When Tricia Smith tried to fill her prescription for tamoxifen two weeks ago , she was shocked by what she heard : The drug commonly prescribed for breast cancer patients is in short supply in Ontario . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Smith , who lives in Kanata , was told by the pharmacy she normally uses that the drug was on back order and not available : They couldn&amp;rsquo ; t fill her prescription . She tried another pharmacy only to be told that they had just eight tablets left ( she usually gets 100 at a time ) . Finally , she found a local drug store that was able to fill her entire prescription . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She was lucky . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There is a shortage of tamoxifen across the country and it could last until the end of January , say officials . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Smith said she wasn&amp;rsquo ; t too concerned when she was originally told that the drug was on back order since her supply wouldn&amp;rsquo ; t run out for a few weeks . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Then I got more concerned , not only for myself but for other women , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For thousands of Ontario patients who have been prescribed the drug to reduce the chance of breast cancer recurring , such a shortage is terrifying . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We take this very seriously , because we know this is very upsetting for patients , &amp;rdquo ; said Dr. Leta Forbes , a medical oncologist and provincial head of Cancer Care Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s systemic treatment program . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Cancer Care Ontario , the Ontario government agency tasked with improving cancer care , and health-care professionals &amp;ldquo ; understand that this is incredibly upsetting for patients and our No. 1 priority is to make sure the patient is supported when we are going through a drug shortage and to give them the information they need , &amp;rdquo ; said Forbes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There have been increasing drug shortages across the country in recent years &amp;mdash ; for a complex variety of reasons . Those shortages have included cancer drugs , including some commonly used for children . At CHEO in Ottawa , two children who needed a drug for acute lymphoblastic leukemia had to miss a treatment because the drug was unavailable . Other children might have to miss treatments in coming weeks , say officials there . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The shortage of tamoxifen will be more widely felt . Around 9,000 people receive it through Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s public drug plan . Many more who are not in the public plan are also prescribed the drug , which is one of the most common cancer treatments . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A kind of hormone treatment therapy , tamoxifen is used to treat breast cancer in patients whose cancer is hormone receptor positive . It works by interfering with the effects of estrogen in the breast tissue and is often prescribed to patients after surgery . Patients usually take it daily for five to 10 years . It is also sometimes used to reduce the chances of breast cancer in high-risk patients as well as in patients with metastatic cancer as well as in some other cancers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It has been on the market for more than 30 years and can reduce cancer recurrence after surgery by nearly 50 per cent , said Forbes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Health Canada has been working to obtain other supplies of the drug after two of the three suppliers to Canada experienced production problems that led to shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Forbes said she is aware of some patients showing up at their pharmacy and being given a smaller supply of pills than their prescription calls for and some pharmacists calling around to try and obtain more supply . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She suggested that patients who have been unable to fill their prescriptions contact their oncologist or family doctor to find an alternative that can be used until tamoxifen is available . It is better for patients to see their doctors and try to find an alternative , she said , rather than going without . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We try to make sure patients get the treatment they need . So if they ran out of their pills and they couldn&amp;rsquo ; t take them for many months then that might be a significant impact , but we are hoping that is not going to be the case because we think the shortage will be shorter than that . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , hospital pharmacies are working to source or find alternatives to a growing list of drugs on back order in Ontario . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The Ottawa Hospital&amp;rsquo ; s pharmacy team works diligently with clinicians in monitoring the supply for a number of drugs , to ensure that we have an adequate supply , &amp;rdquo ; said spokesperson Michaela Schreiter. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; When supply for a particular drug becomes low , they look for alternative suppliers first , followed by other drugs in a similar class , then alternative treatments , when necessary . This has allowed us to maintain quality of care for patients . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Forbes , who herself treats breast cancer patients , said many of them are anxious about the drug shortage . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The last thing we want is for patients to feel anxious about not being able to obtain their medication . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; epayne@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21724;TOOLONG ... reducing your carbon footprint all wrong : The merits of one less child&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21759;TOOLONG ... case for getting fitter -- not slimmer : Why our obsession with weight could be a mistake&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;21794;TOOLONG ... of RCMP officer who helped lead fight against sexual misconduct is denied compensation&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneTue , 12 Nov 2019 11:57:29 **45;21829;TOOLONG ... " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;21947;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1503699 " /&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 100% " height= " 100% " frameborder= " 0 " allowfullscreen= " true " src= " https : **34;21977;TOOLONG ... &gt; &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;p&gt; Short-term staff shortages continue to cause brief suspensions of some service at the Pontiac Hospital . Mostly , these affect obstetrics . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; All surgery was suspended over the past weekend for lack of a surgeon . That meant cancelling obstetrics as well because of the possibility that a woman might need an caesarean delivery . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On Monday , the agency that oversees hospitals in the Outaouais announced that obstetrics in Pontiac is now being suspended until Wednesday morning , this time for lack of nursing staff . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Mothers who are in labour will be redirected to Gatineau , says the Centre inte'gre ' de sante ' et de services sociaux de l&amp;rsquo ; Outaouais . If there&amp;rsquo ; s no time to reach Gatineau , the agency says Pontiac will be able to handle them . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Women who are close to their due dates are being contacted in advance . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Obstetrics has been suspended on other recent occasions at the hospital , usually because of a lack of weekend nurses . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **28;22013;TOOLONG " **35;22043;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " http : //twitter.com/TomSpears1 " &gt;twitt ... &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;22080;TOOLONG ... William John Brown 's death&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;22150;TOOLONG ... things : How the city wants to improve snow and ice clearing this winter&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;nbsp ; &lt;/p&gt; Tom SpearsMon , 11 Nov 2019 23:41:50 **45;22185;TOOLONG ... NewsWomenA century of trauma : Tracing the evolution of PTSD through four soldiershttp : **32;22232;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;22266;TOOLONG ... title= " Pte . Ted Patrick was a signalman ( radio operator ) in the Irish Regiment of Canada . " alt= " Pte . Ted Patrick was a signalman ( radio operator ) in the Irish Regiment of Canada . " data-portal-copyright= " Wayne Cuddington " **28;22302;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500695 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; On the opening morning of the Battle of Vimy Ridge , Capt . Thain MacDowell ran towards enemy lines through a welter of mud and craters , sleet and machine gunfire . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He reached his objective , a German trench , only to find that he had become separated from most of his company . Joined by a pair of army couriers , MacDowell captured two machine gun placements , then climbed down a flight of steps into a deep dugout . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Around a sharp corner , he confronted a roomful of German soldiers . He turned and shouted to an imaginary group of Canadians behind him to hold their grenades . Seventy-seven Germans surrendered . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;22332;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;22374;TOOLONG ... The latest treatment **28;22409;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;22439;TOOLONG ... War army nurse enlisted out of concern for her **28;22474;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;22504;TOOLONG ... composer sets veteran 's poem to music for Remembrance Day&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;22539;TOOLONG ... Alex Kowbel typed his way into the **29;22574;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; He sent the prisoners up the stairs in groups of 12 to conceal the fact they were surrendering to just three Canadian soldiers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They had plenty of rations but we had a great time taking them prisoner , &amp;rdquo ; MacDowell wrote in a dispatch later that day , April 9 , 1917. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; His act of courage and deception would earn him the British Empire&amp;rsquo ; s highest decoration for military valour . One of four Canadians awarded the Victoria Cross at Vimy Ridge , MacDowell was the only one still alive six months later when he returned home to Brockville on sick leave . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; MacDowell was not sick in the conventional sense : He was suffering from what military doctors called &amp;ldquo ; war neurasthenia , &amp;rdquo ; or shellshock . His symptoms included depression , insomnia , headaches , irritability , fever , perspiration , difficulty concentrating and decreased energy . He also had a slight speech impediment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; MacDowell was one of 10,000 Canadian soldiers diagnosed with shellshock during the First World War . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The condition baffled doctors and challenged military leaders , who didn&amp;rsquo ; t know how to deal with the flood of traumatized soldiers that accompanied every major battle . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some attributed the phenomenon to emotional weakness or malingering . More than 300 British and Commonwealth soldiers were executed for cowardice or desertion during the war , including 23 Canadians . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; An unknown number of them suffered from what today we would call post-traumatic stress disorder ( PTSD ) . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; War has inflicted trauma on successive generations of Canadian soldiers whose psychiatric injuries have been variously labelled shellshock , battle exhaustion , combat stress reaction and PTSD . The evolution of that diagnosis has traced a century of conflict : It is a story of service and science and suffering . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;22605;TOOLONG ... title= " First World War Victoria Cross winner Lt.-Col. Thain MacDowell. " alt= " First World War Victoria Cross winner Lt.-Col. Thain MacDowell. " data-portal-copyright= " " **28;22641;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500700 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Capt . Thain MacDowell&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In February 1915 , British psychologist Dr. Charles Myers was the first to identify the phenomenon of shellshock . In the medical journal The Lancet , &lt;a href= " https : **33;22671;TOOLONG ... recounted the case histories of three soldiers&lt;/a&gt; traumatized by shell explosions . Curiously , he said , the soldiers&amp;rsquo ; hearing was little affected , while other functions such as sight , smell , taste and memory were damaged . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The close relation of these cases to those of &amp;lsquo ; hysteria&amp;rsquo ; appears fairly certain , &amp;rdquo ; he concluded . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hysteria was a mental disorder , attributed mostly to women at the time , which typically featured nervousness , fainting or fits . Shellshocked soldiers could be rendered mute or left with partially paralyzed limbs . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some doctors theorized that shellshock was the result of a physical brain injury caused by soldiers&amp;rsquo ; repeated exposure to blast waves from exploding shells &amp;mdash ; a new part of industrialized warfare . Others pointed to afflicted soldiers who had never been in an explosion to argue that the condition was due to a weakness of the nervous system , &amp;ldquo ; neurasthenia , &amp;rdquo ; triggered by the general stress of war . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The world&amp;rsquo ; s &lt;a href= " https : **33;22706;TOOLONG ... personality test&lt;/a&gt; was developed in an attempt to identify recruits with the &amp;ldquo ; emotional instability&amp;rdquo ; that could put them at risk for shellshock. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Treatments were mostly experimental , and sometimes , horrifying . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In December 1916 , Myers spearheaded the establishment of specialized medical units near the frontlines to assess and treat traumatized soldiers using Freudian talk therapy . Theorizing that shellshock was a stress disorder , he believed a patient had to relive his experience to reintegrate the traumatic event with his conscious mind . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A Canadian psychiatrist , Dr. Lewis Yealland , was the leading proponent of an altogether different school of thought : that shellshock was a kind of personal failure . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Yealland , a clinician at London&amp;rsquo ; s National Hospital for the Paralyzed and Epileptic , was convinced he could recondition traumatized soldiers through the power of suggestion . He regularly accompanied those suggestions with powerful electric shocks , and sometimes , cigarette burns . He used electrotherapy on 196 patients during the war , and published his results in a disturbing 1918 book , &lt;a href= " https : **33;22741;TOOLONG ... Disorders of Warfare&lt;/a&gt; . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; No one , including Yealland , could suggest that war hero Thain MacDowell lacked battlefield courage or personal fortitude . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Awarded the Victoria Cross in June 1917 , MacDowell was hospitalized in England and sent home to Canada in October . He spent three months at Brockville General Hospital where , &lt;a href= " http : **30;22776;TOOLONG ? op= ... to his medical history&lt;/a&gt; , he suffered a nervous breakdown . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In January 1918 , after a period of rest , he was deemed fit for service again : &amp;ldquo ; Officer shows much improvement since last examination &amp;mdash ; has regained his emotional control . No attacks of crying since Nov. 17. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; MacDowell returned to England in February 1918 , and spent the year in officer training as the First World War raced towards its bloody conclusion . He returned to Canada in December , one month after the armistice , and again sought help . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A medical history taken at the time says : &amp;ldquo ; Officer states that he tires easily and can not sleep . Has not slept well since Nov. 1916 . He may sleep 3 to 4 hours a night if there has been no excitement . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; ( In November 1916 , MacDowell was thrown into the air by a shell blast at the Somme ; he earned the Distinguished Service Order for his role in capturing three machine gun posts during the same battle. ) &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In January 1919 , MacDowell was made commanding officer of a demobilization unit in Ottawa , where he was also an outpatient at the Sir Sandford Fleming Convalescent Home . He could only manage the work for a few months . By August 1919 , he was an in-patient at Montreal&amp;rsquo ; s Ste. Anne de Bellevue Hospital , complaining of depression , insomnia , restlessness and irritability. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; He tires very easily and in all work loses interest , &amp;rdquo ; reads his medical case history . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Montreal , he was prescribed massage and hydrotherapy , a popular method for treating mental illness in the early 20th century . Warm , continuous baths were used to treat agitated patients ; they were often bathed in a darkened room for hours , sometimes days , at a time . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In October 1919 , after MacDowell was deemed medically unfit for service and discharged , he returned to Ottawa , where he slowly recovered his mental health . His military file does not disclose what treatment he received . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For five years , he worked as private secretary to the minister of defence , and in 1929 , married Norah Hodgson , of Montreal . They had two sons . He later entered the mining business as an investor and executive . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; MacDowell died of a heart attack in March 1960 . He was 69. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;22808;TOOLONG ... title= " Ted Patrick was a signalman ( radio operator ) in the Irish Regiment of Canada . " alt= " Ted Patrick was a signalman ( radio operator ) in the Irish Regiment of Canada . " data-portal-copyright= " Wayne Cuddington " **28;22844;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500685 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Signalman William " Ted " Patrick &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In the Second World War , signalmen maintained communication links between frontline officers and headquarter staff who managed the battlefield. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Enemy forces regularly used signalmen &amp;mdash ; they carried radios on their backs &amp;mdash ; to aim their artillery since they knew officers would be nearby . It made Signalmen William &amp;ldquo ; Ted&amp;rdquo ; Patrick a target in 1944 as he fought his way north through Italy with the Irish Regiment of Canada . He suffered perforated eardrums from shells exploding so close to him . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Patrick&amp;rsquo ; s Italian campaign had other harrowing moments . In the Moro River Valley , he saw a heavily pregnant woman ripped open by a landmine . During another advance , he had to cover the lighted dials of his radio as German soldiers walked past him into an ambush. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He did not seek help for the profound anxiety he suffered . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Infantry soldiers like Patrick were the primary victims of &amp;ldquo ; battle exhaustion&amp;rdquo ; in the Second World War . Research by Canadian military historian Terry Copp , a professor at Wilfrid Laurier University , found that 90 per cent of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s battle exhaustion cases came from ground troops . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In part , that was a reflection of the Royal Canadian Air Force&amp;rsquo ; s uncompromising attitude towards aircrew members who developed psychiatric problems and refused to fly . Such airmen were branded as &amp;ldquo ; lacking in moral fibre&amp;rdquo ; or LMF ; they were often demoted or dishonourably discharged . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Canadian army took a more pragmatic view . Its senior psychiatrist , Col. Frederick Van Nostrand , wanted battle exhausted soldiers treated quickly while close to the frontlines so they could be promptly returned to action . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It meant that as Canadian forces fought through Normandy , hundreds of soldiers were treated each week at field dressing stations for acute battle stress . Typically , they were sedated for 24 hours , given two days of rest , and counselled by a therapist . Many were returned to action only to suffer another breakdown . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Battle exhaustion cases represented one-quarter of all wounded soldiers among Allied forces . &amp;nbsp ; Those numbers caught military planners by surprise in Canada , where medical officers had tried to weed out recruits with emotional instability . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The methods that were used were no better than flipping a coin , &amp;rdquo ; Copp , the author of two books on the history of combat stress , said in an interview . &amp;ldquo ; None of it worked in terms of predicting who would break down or who would not break down under conditions of combat . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In a prophetic report filed at the end of the war , Van Nostrand said he was unsure doctors would ever solve &amp;ldquo ; the vast problem of the psychiatric breakdown of soldiers during war . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It is my opinion , &amp;rdquo ; he wrote , &amp;ldquo ; that the methods now employed in the British , American , and Canadian armies will not materially lower the incidence of psychiatric casualties in a fighting force . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; There are various reasons for these opinions but two of them are fundamental : First , there is direct conflict between the needs of the service and the needs of the individual soldier as assessed by his physician . Secondly , the attitudes and behaviour of the successful soldier are contrary to most of his previous teaching . He must not allow death or mutilation of his comrades to prevent him from reaching his objective , and finally , he must pretend that he is glad to risk his life for that cause . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Van Nostrand pleaded with military planners to accept that normal people can not always manage the exceptional stress of war . &amp;ldquo ; Every soldier has his breaking point , &amp;rdquo ; he warned . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ted Patrick had bumped up against his breaking point . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;22874;TOOLONG ... title= " Ted Patrick . " alt= " Ted Patrick . " data-portal-copyright= " Wayne Cuddington " **28;22910;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500684 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; After the Second World War , Patrick returned home to Ottawa and buried his memories . It was a common approach . He married , took a job in the civil service , and launched a bee-keeping business to supplement his income . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; All the while , however , the tide of war washed over his subconscious . Patrick sometimes attacked his wife in the middle of the night , believing her to be an enemy soldier ; she eventually began to sleep in a separate bedroom . Other times , he&amp;rsquo ; d wake up sobbing . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Patrick scrupulously avoided the legion hall and regimental reunions &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; anything that brought the war to mind . He was also extremely cautious . He didn&amp;rsquo ; t like to go to unfamiliar places ; he was , he said , like a rat that stayed close to a wall . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I would not take a chance on getting hurt or having the family hurt . I was always extremely cautious and went around danger , &amp;rdquo ; he once told an interviewer . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It wasn&amp;rsquo ; t until the 1980s that Patrick was finally diagnosed with PTSD ; his psychiatrist urged him to confront his wartime trauma . Talking about his experiences &amp;mdash ; and sharing his memories &amp;mdash ; eased Patrick&amp;rsquo ; s anxiety . Late in life , he became a dedicated volunteer at the Canadian War Museum and travelled to Holland for ceremonies to commemorate the country&amp;rsquo ; s liberation . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He died in February 2015. &lt;strong&gt; &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;22940;TOOLONG ... title= " Gordon Forbes poses for a photo in his home in Ottawa Tuesday Oct 29 , 2019. " alt= " Gordon Forbes poses for a photo in his home in Ottawa Tuesday Oct 29 , 2019. " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;22976;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500678 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Lt . Gordon Forbes &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Gordon Forbes , 76 , of Orl&amp;eacute ; ans , was on board &lt;a href= " http : **34;23006;TOOLONG ... Kootenay&lt;/a&gt; 50 years ago during the worst peacetime disaster in the history of the Royal Canadian Navy &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; an event that would colour much of his life . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On Oct. 23 , 1969 , H.M.C.S. Kootenay was in the North Atlantic , returning to Halifax from a NATO naval exercise . At 8:21 a.m. , during a full power trial &amp;mdash ; a drill to test the destroyer&amp;rsquo ; s performance at top speed &amp;mdash ; an explosion ripped through the ship&amp;rsquo ; s engine room . A mass of flames shot from a broken gearbox , setting fire to the 10 men inside of the room . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Only three escaped alive . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Thick black smoke quickly filled the lower decks , but its source wasn&amp;rsquo ; t readily apparent to those on the bridge , including Lt. Gordon Forbes , the ship&amp;rsquo ; s weapons officer who was responsible for Kootenay&amp;rsquo ; s 50 tonnes of ammunition . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Engineering Officer Al Kennedy , one of those to escape the engine room , stumbled into the bridge , blackened and badly burned : &amp;ldquo ; Fire in the engine room , &amp;rdquo ; he announced . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That posed problems . The ship&amp;rsquo ; s firefighting equipment was stored near the engine room , and the ship&amp;rsquo ; s main ammunition magazine was immediately behind it . A sailor reported the bulkhead between the two was already hot . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Divers donned tanks and masks to descend into the smoke : They retrieved the ship&amp;rsquo ; s firefighting equipment and rescued sailors trapped by the blinding smoke . Forbes sprayed down the magazine to reduce the threat of a catastrophic explosion . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If the magazine had blown up , it would have destroyed the ship , &amp;rdquo ; he told this newspaper . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It took about three hours to bring the fire under control ; the magazine was then flooded to better stabilize the munitions . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Those on board were told not to talk about the disaster , which had killed nine of their fellow sailors . &amp;ldquo ; No one really knew how to deal with it , &amp;rdquo ; said Forbes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At the time of the Kootenay disaster , the Vietnam War was in full swing : In 1969 , the number of deployed U.S. troops peaked at 549,000. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;23042;TOOLONG ... title= " HMCS Kootenay approaching flight locks , Welland canal . " alt= " HMCS Kootenay approaching flight locks , Welland canal . " data-portal-copyright= " CF Photo Unit " **28;23078;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500686 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Curiously , few soldiers reported battle fatigue symptoms in Vietnam . Army officials attributed that development to limited battlefield exposure : Soldiers were rotated through the war on one-year tours of duty . It gave them a firm date by which their wartime ordeal would end . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Many military planners thought the problem of battlefield stress injuries had been solved . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Instead , of course , it had simply gone to ground . Tens of thousands of soldiers returned from Vietnam traumatized : afflicted by nightmares , insomnia , depression , rage , paranoia and addictions . Psychiatrists labeled the phenomenon delayed psychiatric trauma or &amp;ldquo ; post-Vietnam Syndrome&amp;rdquo ; since some thought the disorder was unique to Vietnam . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It was the first time that psychiatrists recognized that stress injuries were not always immediate , but could announce themselves months , even years , later . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Psychiatrists who worked with Vietnam veterans lobbied to have the disorder formally recognized , and in 1980 , the authoritative American Psychiatric Association made PTSD part of its Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The formal recognition of PTSD had profound consequences and allowed for better diagnosis and treatment . Therapists recognized the same disorder in Holocaust survivors , sexual assault victims , first responders &amp;mdash ; and others afflicted by traumatic events such as the Kootenay explosion . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sub-Lieut . Clark Reiffenstein , one of those who donned scuba gear to plunge into the ship&amp;rsquo ; s smoke-filled lower decks , died of suicide one month after the fire . He was posthumously awarded the Star of Courage . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Many other Kootenay sailors struggled with alcoholism , nightmares and anxiety . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 1990 , Gordon Forbes was diagnosed with clinical depression soon after retiring from the navy . He suffered from paranoia and had difficulty sleeping . His condition was later linked to PTSD and recognized as a service injury by Veterans Affairs Canada . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It was one measure of the advance in the military&amp;rsquo ; s approach to psychiatric infirmity . &amp;nbsp ; &amp;ldquo ; I was very pleased that they started to recognize PTSD , &amp;rdquo ; he said , &amp;ldquo ; and not just throw people out of the service on medical grounds , which is what happened to people who went to get help after the Kootenay fire . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2010 , Forbes published a book , We Are As One , about the disaster and its emotional aftermath . Researching and writing the book , he said , was a cathartic experience for everyone involved : &amp;ldquo ; I had so many men come up to me after I wrote the book and say , &amp;lsquo ; I thought I was the only one . ' &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;23108;TOOLONG ... title= " Natacha Dupuis is a former Canadian soldier and Afghan vet who suffered debilitating PTSD after her war service . " alt= " Natacha Dupuis is a former Canadian soldier and Afghan vet who suffered debilitating PTSD after her war service . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;23144;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500687 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Master Cpl . Natacha Dupuis&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ten years ago , in March 2009 , Master Cpl . Natacha Dupuis was put in charge of her first mission with the reconnaissance squadron of the Royal Canadian Dragoons : a week-long foot patrol in Kandahar Province . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Qualified as a tank gunner , she had served in Bosnia and was on her second tour of duty in Afghanistan . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Leaving the Forward Operating Base Frontenac , a military outpost near Kandahar , Dupuis led her patrol to a hilltop camp . The next morning , as the patrol departed , a massive explosion ripped into the armoured vehicle behind Dupuis&amp;rsquo ; vehicle . The 14-tonne Coyote was blown into the air and landed on its roof . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dupuis helped to collect the remains of two dead soldiers while preparing for a secondary attack that never came . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Her team was quickly evacuated , but Dupuis kept reliving the incident and re-imagining the terrible scene . For days , unable to turn off her flow of adrenaline , she couldn&amp;rsquo ; t sleep . A psychiatrist prescribed her sleeping pills , but she struggled through each day and often sought out a private place to cry . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She willed herself through her final two months of service in Afghanistan . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As soon as she returned home to Petawawa , however , she fell apart : Dupuis suffered powerful flashbacks and panic attacks that left her gasping for air . Diagnosed with PTSD , she transferred to Ottawa , then took a leave . She left the military for good in 2014. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Her story is a familiar one . A &lt;a href= " https : **33;23174;TOOLONG ... Affairs Canada report last year&lt;/a&gt; revealed that about 16 per cent of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s Afghan veterans &amp;mdash ; more than 6,700 soldiers &amp;mdash ; have been diagnosed with PTSD. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The PTSD rate remained high in Afghanistan even though the military had tried to carefully prepare soldiers for the stress of war . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Soldiers were briefed about the nature of stress injuries and the importance of seeking early treatment . Those leaving Afghanistan were given an overseas decompression period and repeatedly screened for PTSD or related afflictions . But the &amp;ldquo ; psychological inoculation&amp;rdquo ; of soldiers did little to reduce the overall incidence of PTSD. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I personally feel I was absolutely ready to face going to Afghanistan , &amp;rdquo ; said Dupuis. &amp;ldquo ; I was given very good training and it showed . We were able to react to the IED attack . But how do you prepare people to see a horrible scene like that ? &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; After her diagnosis , Dupuis explored a variety of treatments , including cognitive therapy , a kind of talk therapy designed to help patients identify negative patterns in the way they perceive and deal with everyday events . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; At Montfort Hospital , she tried a newly developed treatment , &lt;a href= " https : **33;23209;TOOLONG ... movement desensitization and reprocessing&lt;/a&gt; ( EMDR ) , which had been shown to help people process their traumatic memories through a series of guided , rapid eye movements . The therapy is believed to mimic the beneficial effects of rapid-eye movement ( REM ) sleep , which plays a role in the healthy storage of emotional memories . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; EMDR was really difficult , &amp;rdquo ; said Dupuis. &amp;ldquo ; It would drain me a lot because it takes you right back to the trauma : You would feel like you are still there . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dupuis worked with a massage therapist , who helped her with meditation and breathing techniques , and also became involved with Soldier On , an organization devoted to helping injured soldiers through athletics . She co-captained Team Canada at the 2017 Invictus Games in Toronto , and won three gold medals . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; For me , I think the big breakthrough came with sport &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; but I had to be ready for that breakthrough , &amp;rdquo ; Dupuis said . &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s a very complex recovery process . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Although she still suffers the occasional flashback &amp;mdash ; fireworks or big crowds can trigger them &amp;mdash ; Dupuis has returned to work as a peer support co-ordinator with Operational Stress Injury Social Support , a joint program of Veterans Affairs Canada and the Department of National Defence . She helps soldiers and veterans with PTSD. &amp;ldquo ; I share my experience , &amp;rdquo ; she said , &amp;ldquo ; and try to give them hope that it is possible to have a better life . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s most decorated soldiers have suffered the effects of PTSD , including &lt;a href= " https : **33;23244;TOOLONG ... William Barker&lt;/a&gt; , &lt;a href= " https : **33;23279;TOOLONG ... Prince&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href= " https : **33;23314;TOOLONG ... Dallaire&lt;/a&gt; . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;23349;TOOLONG ... title= " Retired General , Senator Romeo Dallaire. " alt= " Retired General , Senator Romeo Dallaire. " data-portal-copyright= " Peter Bregg " **28;23385;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1500698 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; It underlines the fact that a century after shellshock was first described in a medical journal , doctors still can&amp;rsquo ; t predict who will suffer PTSD when exposed to the horrors of war , or why some soldiers are affected and not others . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Through statistical analysis , researchers know women are more likely than men to develop PTSD ; that people with pre-existing psychiatric conditions are more vulnerable ; and that risk increases with each additional overseas deployment a soldier undertakes. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Scientists are also slowly coming to understand what actually happens in the brain of those with PTSD : Research suggests the amygdala , an almond-shaped region of the brain that processes emotions , is overactive along with parts of the prefrontal cortex that promote fear . They&amp;rsquo ; re now examining PTSD-altered brains on a neuronal level , which raises the potential for targeted new treatments . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dupuis , however , said soldiers experience PTSD so differently that it&amp;rsquo ; s hard to believe there will ever be a single cure &amp;mdash ; other than an end to war . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The brain , I think , has its limits : I think there&amp;rsquo ; s only so much a human can take . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;23415;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;23457;TOOLONG ... The latest treatment **28;23492;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;23522;TOOLONG ... War army nurse enlisted out of concern for her **28;23557;TOOLONG &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;23587;TOOLONG ... composer sets veteran 's poem to music for Remembrance Day&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;23622;TOOLONG ... Alex Kowbel typed his way into the **29;23657;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; Andrew DuffyFri , 08 Nov 2019 15:05:47 **45;23688;TOOLONG ... **31;23735;TOOLONG treatment an unprecedented leap forward in addressing damage after heart attackhttp : **34;23768;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;23804;TOOLONG ... title= " Veronika Sedlakova , post-doctoral fellow ( L ) , Sarah Mclaughlin , PhD(c) , and Dr. Emilio Alarcon , principal investigator and laboratory director , University of Ottawa Heart Institute , have developed a recombinant human collagen matrix for cardiac tissue repair . October 24 , 2019. " alt= " Veronika Sedlakova , post-doctoral fellow ( L ) , Sarah Mclaughlin , PhD(c) , and Dr. Emilio Alarcon , principal investigator and laboratory director , University of Ottawa Heart Institute , have developed a recombinant human collagen matrix for cardiac tissue repair . October 24 , 2019. " data-portal-copyright= " Errol McGihon " **28;23840;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1491854 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; It took years for Emilio Alarcon and his University of Ottawa Heart Institute research team to fully believe what they were seeing &amp;mdash ; that a gel they had developed containing human collagen was repairing damaged hearts in mice . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; They repeated their research study several times , coming up with randomized , blinded results , just to make sure the findings were correct . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We had to complete it many times because we couldn&amp;rsquo ; t believe it , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The results of that work , five years in the making , were published in the prestigious journal Nature Communications on Friday . Alarcon and Erik Suuronen , a scientist in the division of cardiac surgery and director of its biomaterials and regeneration program , are lead authors . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The injectable material is the first in the world prepared using human collagen and is being called an unprecedented leap forward in addressing repair of cardiac muscle after a heart attack . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The work is still years away from clinical use on humans , but the findings are expected to attract attention because of the potential of the gel , which is designed to be injected into the hearts of patients with damaged heart tissue after a heart attack . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Alarcon said his team believes it performs better than any of the cell-based therapies or drug treatments currently available . The treatment works , he said , by increasing the number of cardiac muscle cells and blood capillaries in the tissue around damaged areas of the heart . The gel also helps bring more wound-healing cells to the site to promote repair . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Heart diseases are the leading cause of death around the world , and coronary artery disease , which can lead to a heart attack , is the most common . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; About 10 per cent of people who have a heart attack ( or myocardial infarction ) will develop scarring and thickening of the heart wall that can lead to heart failure , which has a high mortality rate and its treatment is costly to the health system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Alarcon noted that patients in remote areas without easy access to health care and those who don&amp;rsquo ; t seek immediate treatment or don&amp;rsquo ; t know they had a heart attack are more likely to suffer heart muscle damage . The treatment being developed by the heart institute team restored heart function in mice with scarred cardiac muscle . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The heart institute&amp;rsquo ; s BioEngineering and Therapeutic Solutions ( BEaTS ) team &amp;ldquo ; are hopeful their human collagen gel will one day lead to a recovery of heart function and prevent heart failure in humans , &amp;rdquo ; said Alarcon , &amp;ldquo ; but more testing is required . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The study published Friday is a first step toward that potential treatment , he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Canada , approximately 600,000 patients live with advanced heart failure and health-care costs amount to more than $2.8 billion every year . As people live longer , both the number of patients and the cost of treatments are likely to increase . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;23870;TOOLONG ... airline fares are the best way to score super cheap plane tickets . NewsMenSeniorsWomenHow the elderly and frail are caught in the crosshairs of push to end hallway medicinehttp : **32;24022;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;24056;TOOLONG ... title= " Melanie Dea 's husband Richard Martin , 75 , was diagnosed with Huntington 's Disease 12 years ago . " alt= " Melanie Dea 's husband Richard Martin , 75 , was diagnosed with Huntington 's Disease 12 years ago . She was recently handed a bill for his care from the Montfort Hospital after she refused to take a spot for him in a facility with a woeful reputation in an Alzheimer 's unit . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;24092;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1485151 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hospitals are not the right place for them and their families can&amp;rsquo ; t care for them at home . The elderly and frail are increasingly collateral damage in the drive to end hallway medicine in Ontario , say advocates and families . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Patients who occupy hospital beds but no longer need acute care , ALC &amp;mdash ; alternative level of care &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; patients are a key factor in hospital overcrowding . But with record waiting lists for long-term care beds and shortages of home care workers , patients and their families say they are caught in the middle and feeling pressured. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This is a crisis , &amp;rdquo ; said Melanie Dea of Rockland , who recently experienced that pressure first hand . Her husband Richard Martin , who has Huntington&amp;rsquo ; s disease , was treated at Montfort Hospital for pneumonia in July . By August he had improved , but was on a waiting list for long-term care and Dea could not safely care for him at home . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She said the hospital suggested he go to an Alzheimer&amp;rsquo ; s unit of a long-term care home . Rea refused because her husband does not have dementia . The hospital began charging him $62.18 a day , a co-payment she says she will not pay . He has since moved to a long-term care home . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , the wife of an elderly patient in the same hospital room was in tears after being told her husband was being discharged , said Dea . The man ended up in the hospital because he was wandering the streets at night and his wife could no longer care for him . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Trevor Mertz of Chesterville , says his mother-in-law felt pressured to move into an Ottawa long-term care home by staff at Winchester Hospital when she was there in 2017. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They said , &amp;lsquo ; You have two hours to decide or the spot will be gone . ' &amp;rdquo ; Her stay at the home , with a history of health and safety violations , was a &amp;ldquo ; nightmare&amp;rdquo ; , according to Mertz . She eventually moved to another home , but died soon after . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; You shouldn&amp;rsquo ; t pressure people on a Friday , saying you have two hours to make a decision . If I had seen the place , I would have said no. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Jane Meadus of the Toronto-based Advocacy Centre for the Elderly said her organization hears from families on a daily basis who are distraught about having to quickly find a solution for a frail relative being discharged from hospital . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; They come to us in tears . It is our biggest thing right now and it is just heartbreaking . It has always happened , but the pressure on people is worse now . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meadus said some patients are being illegally prevented from applying for long-term care from hospital or forced into retirement homes to wait until a less expensive long-term care bed becomes available . &amp;ldquo ; We have got two-tier medicine on the backs of seniors , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Hospital officials , meanwhile , say the hospital is not where frail and elderly patients in need of chronic care should be . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Cholly Boland , CEO of Winchester Hospital , would not discuss individual cases , but said the hospital&amp;rsquo ; s philosophy is that it is not good to be in a hospital if you don&amp;rsquo ; t need to be . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If you are a person within the ALC category , by definition you do not need to be in the hospital and in general , it is not a good place to be . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Montfort Hospital spokesperson Genevi&amp;egrave ; ve Picard said patients are charged a co-payment when they are waiting in hospital for a long-term care bed , according to provincial policy . The preference , though , is for them to apply from home . &amp;ldquo ; Research has demonstrated that it is easier for people to make important decisions for the next stage while they are in their regular environment and can validate if they can safely remain in their home . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She said she is aware of cases in which people have felt pressured to leave , but added patients will get better care tailored to their needs at home with service providers , in a retirement home or long-term care home . &amp;ldquo ; We know that situations such as these are stressful times for the patients and their loved ones . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Leah Levesque , head of nursing at Queensway Carleton Hospital , acknowledged that the transition from hospital to home or institutional care can be hard on families . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I think the bottom line for us is we think patients should be in the right bed getting appropriate care from the most appropriate providers . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That can be easier said than done , though . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The average wait in the Ottawa area for long-term care was 186 days in 2017 , above the provincial average of 146 days . In addition , support worker shortages and increasing demand mean home care is not always available or reliable . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Alan Forster , vice president of innovation and quality at The Ottawa Hospital , said making sure ALC patients get appropriate care is a societal issue . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If we continue to use hospitals as the place of last resort for people and don&amp;rsquo ; t figure out an alternative for people who are frail and in need of close attention , if we don&amp;rsquo ; t make places for that part of the population , then it will get worse for individuals who are in that situation and increasingly difficult for folks not in that situation . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are currently between 150 and 200 ALC patients at The Ottawa Hospital on any given day . Montfort has seen a 75 per cent increase in ALC patients in the past three years . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meadus , meanwhile , said her organization sees daily evidence that families and patients are bearing the brunt of the push to end hallway medicine . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We see people being sent home , families being told to mortgage their house to pay for parents&amp;rsquo ; care in a retirement home , &amp;rdquo ; she said . The Advocacy Centre for the Elderly also sees seniors being discharged to homeless shelters , motels and transitional homes . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Everyone talks about hallway medicine and those taking up the beds should be in long-term care . But no one ever talks about the effects on those people . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;24122;TOOLONG ... use admission at U.S. border snagging Canadian boomers , says lawyer&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;24157;TOOLONG ... 10 hours or more a day can lead to greater risks of stroke , study finds&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;24192;TOOLONG ... is the limit ' for heart treatment without invasive surgery&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneWed , 16 Oct 2019 10:43:45 **45;24227;TOOLONG ... NewsMenSeniorsWomen'Sky is the limit ' for heart treatment without invasive surgeryhttp : **33;24274;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;24309;TOOLONG ... title= " The team involved in pioneering a new procedure that , instead of open heart surgery , fixes a deadly heart arrhythmia with a non-invasive procedure that takes only minutes includes Professor of Cardiology Dr. Andrew Crean , Associate Professor of Cardiology Dr. Calum Redpath and Radiation Oncologist Dr. Graham Cook . " alt= " The team involved in pioneering a new procedure that , instead of open heart surgery , fixes a deadly heart arrhythmia with a non-invasive procedure that takes only minutes includes Professor of Cardiology Dr. Andrew Crean , Associate Professor of Cardiology Dr. Calum Redpath and Radiation Oncologist Dr. Graham Cook . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;24345;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1482574 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; When the treatment was done , the patient looked confused . &amp;ldquo ; Is that it ? &amp;rdquo ; he asked Dr. Calum Redpath. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That was it . The potentially lifesaving treatment for a deadly heart ailment was completed in a painless , non-invasive session . The patient was free to go . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Performed by a team of doctors from the University of Ottawa Heart Institute in partnership with doctors at The Ottawa Hospital cancer centre , the treatment done in late September was a first in Canada . The Heart Institute is now one of the few centres in the world capable of performing it . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This new technique offers patients a treatment option that does not require the body to be opened in any way , &amp;rdquo ; Redpath said . &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s a real game-changing innovation , almost a fairytale . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Although it is still experimental and being offered on a compassionate basis , Redpath believes the &amp;ldquo ; sky is the limit&amp;rdquo ; for the ways the procedure could change treatment of a common and often deadly heart arrhythmia called ventricular tachycardia , including enabling it to be performed on more patients in smaller centres using equipment used to treat cancer patients . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;24375;TOOLONG ... title= " The team behind the breakthrough procedure in Ottawa includes , left to right , Professor of Cardiology Dr. Andrew Crean , Associate Professor of Cardiology Dr. Calum Redpath , medical physicist Katie Lekx-Toniolo and Radiation Oncologist Dr. Graham Cook . " alt= " The team behind the breakthrough procedure in Ottawa includes , left to right , Professor of Cardiology Dr. Andrew Crean , Associate Professor of Cardiology Dr. Calum Redpath , medical physicist Katie Lekx-Toniolo and Radiation Oncologist Dr. Graham Cook . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;24411;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1482623 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sudden cardiac death kills around 50,000 Canadians every year . Most of those deaths are caused by ventricular arrhythmia , the condition that is the focus of the new non-invasive treatment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Conventional treatment for ventricular arrhythmia involves surgery : usually through a catheter inserted in an artery or vein in the patient&amp;rsquo ; s groin . Once inside , radio-frequency energy or other methods are used to destroy the damaged area of the patient&amp;rsquo ; s heart muscle that is causing rapid and irregular heartbeats . That ablation , as it is known , is done to restore a regular heartbeat . More than 150 such surgeries are performed at the Heart Institute every year . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The new non-invasive procedure first performed in Ottawa last month could end the need for traditional heart surgery to treat the common and often deadly heart arrhythmia . Instead of time in an operating room , anesthesia , pain medicine and days of hospitalization and recovery , the patient who underwent the treatment last month put his shirt back on and walked home . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Current drug treatment for the condition are either ineffective or have side-effects , and the invasive procedures , including catheter ablation surgery or implanting a defibrillator , require prolonged surgery with potential complications and sometimes long recoveries. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The procedure that uses two novel therapies , took place in late September . First , the patient underwent electrocardiographic imaging , in which doctors mapped the electrical activity of his heart using advanced imaging technology . During that procedure , the patient was fully awake and conscious , said Dr. Andrew Crean , co-director of the cardiac MRI service at the Heart Institute . He works with Redpath to identify damaged heart tissue causing arrhythmias. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Using that map , a radiation oncologist burned off damaged areas of the heart muscle using technology normally used to treat cancer patients . No surgery was needed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We&amp;rsquo ; re excited to use this treatment now on portions of the heart , with the hope that , with just one 30-minute treatment , our current patients will live longer and eventually contribute to research studies so we can learn more about who can benefit the most from this and by how much , said Dr. Graham Cook , a radiation oncologist working on the treatment . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Redpath says he expects up to six patients to receive the treatment in Ottawa in coming months . They are all patients who are unable or unwilling to pursue conventional treatments . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; So far , 30 patients have been treated , mostly through Washington University in St. Louis , where it was developed . Among those patients , there is a 94 per cent success rate . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But , before it can be widely used , the treatment will be studied to determine whether it is worthwhile . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Redpath said the procedure is significantly less costly than conventional treatment &amp;mdash ; maybe five times less than surgery . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; If it were to become a routine treatment , he said , it could change how many patients were treated by allowing smaller hospitals to treat patients , even using it as a preventative procedure . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The sky is the limit . There is really no limit to the number of patients that could be treated , &amp;rdquo ; Redpath said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; epayne@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;24441;TOOLONG ... company that made Ottawa 's LRT trains wo n't discuss them&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;24476;TOOLONG ... have Michelin stars evaded Canada 's top restaurants ? &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;24511;TOOLONG ... with child labour ? " data-portal-copyright= " Desmond Boylan " **28;24703;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1434031 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; An Ontario businessman who travelled to Cuba in 2017 could be the first private Canadian citizen to have symptoms consistent with Havana syndrome , the mysterious illness that has affected diplomats and their families who were posted there . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The man , who has asked to remain anonymous because he is worried about his safety , says he experienced what he calls an &amp;ldquo ; attack&amp;rdquo ; while in his hotel room in downtown Havana in early 2017 . He was there as part of a privately sponsored trade mission . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He was sitting in his hotel room when he experienced a sensation inside his head that he has a hard time describing more than two years later . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I have never experienced anything like it . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The man said he didn&amp;rsquo ; t hear any sounds , but felt overwhelming pressure and sensations in his head . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I felt pressure . It was like a gripping arrest of your consciousness , something being thrust on me , but I couldn&amp;rsquo ; t escape it . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The man described the experience as sudden and terrifying . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I had no idea what the source was , but I knew it was abnormal . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When he could stand up from his chair , he was exhausted . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I thought , &amp;lsquo ; I have to lie down , I don&amp;rsquo ; t know what is going on&amp;rsquo ; . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He eventually left the hotel in an attempt to get some fresh air and clear his head , which was in a &amp;ldquo ; mental fog&amp;rdquo ; . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While outside , he says he had a second experience with the sensations of pressure , but this time not as strong . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He returned to the hotel and slept for more than three hours . Because he was so terrified by the strange experience , he says , he was relieved when he woke up that he was still alive . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The man describes a severe &amp;ldquo ; brain fog&amp;rdquo ; that got worse in the days following the incident . He also began suffering from dizziness and tinnitus among many other symptoms . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It got worse to the point that I had dizzy spells in the weeks and months that followed . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; It wasn&amp;rsquo ; t until he read an article in his local newspaper about the mysterious syndrome affecting Canadian and U.S. diplomats that the man began to wonder whether his experience was related . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; His brief trip to Havana happened at the same time diplomats and their families experienced brain injuries from sources that have yet to be identified . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The man made an appointment at the University of Miami where some Canadian and American diplomats affected by Havana syndrome had been examined and tested . After a series of tests , the Ontario businessman was told his results were consistent with many other people who complained of similar attacks while in Cuba . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He also called the RCMP and was interviewed in Ottawa . This newspaper has seen a copy of the statement he sent RCMP in 2018. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Over the summer ( after returning from Cuba ) , I experienced almost every symptom that has been reported in the press and the symptoms were severe : dizziness , hearing loss , fatigue , weakness , difficulty concentrating , short-term memory loss , difficulty sleeping . &amp;rdquo ; He added that he was having difficulty managing his business responsibilities . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; John Babcock , spokesperson for Global Affairs Canada , said an RCMP investigation is ongoing in collaboration with Cuban authorities . He added that there is &amp;ldquo ; &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt;no evidence that Canadian travellers to Cuba are at risk . Canadian travellers should continue to consult the Government of Canada 's travel advice and advisories for the latest updates . " &lt;/span&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Researchers in the U.S. and Canada have confirmed that diplomats and others posted to Havana suffered unusual brain injuries that are affecting their balance , hearing , vision , cognitive abilities and more . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Recently , research led by Dr. Alon Friedman of Dalhousie University&amp;rsquo ; s Brain Repair Centre , &lt;a href= " https : **33;24733;TOOLONG ... that pesticides used to combat mosquito-spread Zika virus could be responsible for the brain injuries&lt;/a&gt; suffered by Canadians and Americans posted to Havana . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That theory is controversial among some of the diplomats who are suing the Canadian government for $28 million , saying they failed to protect them or to take proper action to treat them . The diplomats , through their lawyer , say they are frustrated that there is no clear answer about what caused their illnesses , or any definitive answer about whether they were targeted with some kind of weapon . The insecticide theory , they say , is not conclusive. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Others have proposed that the diplomats could be victims of some kind of sonic weapon , although it is not clear why or by whom . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ontario businessman said he was not aware of spraying inside or around the hotel room where he was staying . Nor did he have any explanation for why he would be the target of what he believes was an attack . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; He has not heard anything back after speaking with RCMP officials . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; And the man said the many stories about the issue and his own ongoing , but improving , symptoms have left him anxious and stressed .
