NewsMenWomenWorldDoctors Without Borders says not enough people are getting vaccinated in Ebola outbreakhttp : **32;24985;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;25019;TOOLONG ... title= " A child reacts as a health worker injects her with the Ebola vaccine , in Goma , Democratic Republic of Congo , August 5 , 2019. " alt= " A child reacts as a health worker injects her with the Ebola vaccine , in Goma , Democratic Republic of Congo , August 5 , 2019. " data-portal-copyright= " Baz Ratner " **28;25055;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1480048 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; With Ebola deaths mounting in the Democratic Republic of Congo , the aid agency Doctors Without Borders is raising alarm bells about the way the vaccine to prevent the disease is being administered there . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Jason Nickerson , humanitarian affairs adviser with Doctors Without Borders , said the organization is increasingly concerned that not enough people in DRC are being vaccinated to get the deadly outbreak under control . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Added to the frustration , he said , is lack of transparency around the vaccine , which was produced by Canadian scientists at the National Microbiology Laboratory in Winnipeg . The vaccine is now licensed to pharmaceutical company Merck , which is in the process of getting it approved and regulated . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , it is being used during the Ebola outbreak in the DRC on a compassionate basis . So far , more than 2,000 people have died in what is the second-worst Ebola outbreak the world has seen , after the West African outbreak , which lasted from 2014 until 2016. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Nickerson , who has recently returned to Ottawa from DRC , said Doctors Without Borders wants to see more transparency around how the vaccine is being rolled out by the World Health Organization there . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; For a long time , one of the big problems with this vaccine is that there has been very little transparency into many aspects of it . For a long time , we didn&amp;rsquo ; t know how many doses there were globally . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Given that , Nickerson said it concerns Doctors Without Borders that only 500 people are being vaccinated a day when the outbreak is not under control . So far , more than 200,000 people have been vaccinated. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One of the complicating factors is that vaccination is still being done using the so-called ring technique in which contacts of people with the virus are vaccinated in a ring around them to hinder the spread of the disease . The technique was used to eradicate smallpox . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Nickerson said the strategy is rigid . As well , contact tracing is slow and often incomplete , especially when cases continue to turn up in the community . One third of all new Ebola cases occur in people who are not being tracked in the community . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Increasing the number of people being vaccinated could make a difference in the deadly outbreak . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We now have the vaccine and I think that is one tool in the tool box , but it needs to be implemented alongside other public health interventions . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; This is the first major outbreak for which the Ebola vaccine &amp;mdash ; although still experimental &amp;mdash ; has been available from the beginning . It has had a success rate of around 97 per cent in DRC , said Nickerson. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Experimental antiviral treatments have also been successfully used in DRC. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;LISTEN TO THE 10/3 PODCAST&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;iframe src= " **40;25085;TOOLONG ... " data-portal-copyright= " Tony Caldwell " **28;25275;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1478509 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; An Orl&amp;eacute ; ans woman says her husband developed bed sores after homecare workers failed to show up to care for him , leaving him in soiled briefs for 30 hours at a time . When caregivers did show up , they were told by their agency not to move him , so he has remained in his bed for weeks . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Mireille Landry said she has now been advised by the Champlain LHIN ( Local Health Integration Network ) , which is in charge of coordinating home care in the region , to pay for respite care for her husband , who suffers from dementia , until the situation can be sorted out . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I am tired of this , &amp;rdquo ; said Landry , who said she has spent weeks dealing with a homecare agency and the LHIN because of personal support workers either failing to show up or being unable to move her husband in and out of bed when they did get there . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Her concerns come as the LHIN acknowledges it is struggling to maintain home care amid personal support worker shortages . PSWs , as they are known , do the bulk of home care work in the province . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In a statement , the LHIN&amp;rsquo ; s interim CEO Catherine Butler said the demand for homecare services is high and increasing each year . Against that backdrop , the shortage of PSWs is affecting the local health system . She said the impact of the shortage is worse in areas outside of urban Ottawa . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; More people are receiving homecare services than before . However , the provincewide shortage of personal support workers is affecting our local health system , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We understand the additional stress and deeply regret the problems this has created for patients and their caregivers. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Landry blames sloppy management practices on the part of the agencies in charge of providing home care for some of the problems . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;25305;TOOLONG ... title= " Mireille Landry poses for a photo in her home in Ottawa Friday Oct 4 , 2019. When she attempted to , he nearly fell and Landry , who has had spinal fractures , had to help the PSW get him back into bed . After that , she said , PSWs working for the agency were told not to try to move him . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Landry said she lost faith in the agency , called Access Care , but after a meeting agreed to continue with them . Subsequently , she said , she was told they would no longer provide care for her husband and she would have to pay for respite care or make do until the situation was sorted out . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As of Friday , Landry was told PSWs would come to her house until the end of the weekend , at which point she would have to wait for a new agency . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Neither Access nor the LHIN would discuss the specific case . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Landry&amp;rsquo ; s husband was diagnosed with progressive dementia in 2015 after a legal career that lasted more than 50 years . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; After spending months in the hospital , he returned to the couple&amp;rsquo ; s Orl&amp;eacute ; ans home with the help of regular visits from support workers who visited three times a day to provide in-person care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But that system began to fall apart in late summer , said Landry , and she began to worry that PSWs weren&amp;rsquo ; t being trained properly to move her husband . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She said the situation has left her to handle the &amp;ldquo ; mess&amp;rdquo ; created by sloppy management practices by homecare agencies , which make an already tough situation worse . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; On the one hand , everybody is saying we want to keep people home as long as we can , but on the other hand , they are not doing their best . My husband is not even getting the basic care . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The province should pay support workers more to do their jobs , she said , noting that PSWs who take care of more complex patients like her husband do not receive additional pay and are less likely to take on the work . Also , PSWs who work in remote areas are not paid for travel time . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The LHIN says personal support worker shortages are more pronounced in rural Eastern Ontario than in Ottawa . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Every day , 10,000 people receive direct home care visits provided by the Champlain LHIN . Those services include helping people with daily activities , nursing and therapy . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The LHIN said it is continuing to work with agencies and contracted providers who employ PSWs to develop solutions to shortages . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The province committed more than $8.4 million on Friday to expanding home and community care in Ottawa . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;25371;TOOLONG ... " data-portal-copyright= " Joe Raedle " **28;25623;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1474693 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada is not immune to the kind of measles outbreaks alarming health officials around the globe , an Ottawa pediatric infectious disease expert is warning . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Anne Pham-Huy , an infectious disease physician at CHEO and member of the National Advisory Committee on Immunization , said less-than-optimal immunization rates at a time of dramatically increasing global outbreaks means Canada , like other countries , is at risk . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Pham-Huy made the comments as Canada is on track to have a serious year for measles , with 111 cases since January , compared with 29 cases in all of 2018 . There were 19 new cases during one week in August alone . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada and many countries eliminated the highly contagious disease as a result of mass-immunization campaigns , although it has remained endemic in parts of the world . But , as a result of vaccine refusal and dropping immunization rates in developing countries , some countries are now losing their measles elimination status &amp;mdash ; the UK among them . France , meanwhile , has among the highest rates of vaccine distrust in the world . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The U.S. , which has had more than 1,200 measles cases so far this year , compared with 371 in 2018 , now faces the risk of losing its measles elimination status . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Does that predict the direction in which Canada is going ? &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Public Health Agency of Canada spokesperson Anna Madison said the agency does not foresee Canada losing its measles elimination status . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Although measles outbreaks have occurred in Canada over the years and more recently because of imported cases , sustained transmission of measles within Canada has not been observed since Canada&amp;rsquo ; s measles elimination status was achieved in 1998. &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The number of cases fluctuates every year , Maddison said , as the number of travellers bringing measles into Canada varies , depending on where they go , whether they are immunized and the measles activity in other countries . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Still , Canada&amp;rsquo ; s immunization rates are high , but not high enough when it comes to completely protecting against measles , said Pham-Huy . Canada&amp;rsquo ; s rate of immunization against measles is around 90 per cent for seven year olds , she said , with pockets of higher and lower coverage . Public health officials want to see coverage rates of 95 per cent to thwart the spread of the highly infectious disease . That is doubly important at a time when there are so many cases of measles around the world and when global travel is so common . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As with the U.S. , where one measles outbreak is centred in an Orthodox Jewish community in New York , Canada has communities where vaccination rates are low . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Quebec a religious community in Lanaudi&amp;egrave ; re north of Montreal , which discourages vaccination , was the centre of a measles outbreak in 2015 . One case was brought to the community after a trip to Disneyland in California and it eventually ballooned to 157 people , the bulk of the country&amp;rsquo ; s 196 cases that year . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **33;25653;TOOLONG : &lt;a href= " https : **33;25688;TOOLONG ... at risk of pandemics that could kill millions , panel warns&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2014 , there were even more cases &amp;mdash ; a total of 418 spread across five provinces . The largest outbreak that year was in a non-immunizing religious community in British Columbia . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We are not immune , &amp;rdquo ; said Pham-Huy. &amp;ldquo ; There are pockets of communities that are under vaccinated . If you do introduce a case , because of its highly infectious nature , this is the recipe for outbreaks . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Pham-Huy said public health officials also worry about vaccine hesitancy in Canada . The World Health Organization named vaccine hesitancy as one of the top threats to global health . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In general , immunization rates in Canada are not at the target , but they are good . However ( people who are vaccine hesitant ) , are a minority , but it seems to be an increasingly vocal group . It is important that families get the right information from the right source . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Earlier this week , anti-vaxxers protested vocally when Toronto Public Health passed a motion asking the province to mandate vaccinations for all schoolchildren . Exemptions for health and other reasons are currently available . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Pham-Huy sometimes meet with parents who are hesitant about vaccinations. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The bottom-line message is that the vaccine is safe and it is effective against one of the most contagious infections that exists . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; During the first six months of 2019 , there were more measles cases reported worldwide &amp;mdash ; 364,808 &amp;mdash ; &amp;nbsp ; than in any year since 2006. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Maddison of the Public Health Agency of Canada added that it is &amp;ldquo ; concerning to see vaccine-preventable diseases , particularly those as serious and highly contagious as measles , in Canada and around the globe . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; A vaccination coverage rate of 95 per cent is needed to prevent outbreaks of the most serious and contagious of vaccine-preventable diseases , &amp;rdquo ; said Maddison . &amp;ldquo ; Canada continues to fall short of the 95 per cent coverage goals set by the federal and provincial governments . When immunization rates do not meet the levels needed for community immunity , outbreaks of disease such as measles can and have happened . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The best protection against measles is vaccination with two doses of measles-containing vaccine . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **27;25723;TOOLONG " **34;25752;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;25788;TOOLONG ... the stigma : Daughter wants officer who died by suicide recognized&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;25823;TOOLONG ... questions DND report on conduct of employee who was not allowed to see or respond to allegations against her&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;25858;TOOLONG ... do n't recognize heart attacks when symptoms come on slowly&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneMon , 30 Sep 2019 10:38:22 **45;25893;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal **28;25940;TOOLONG youth identified as Canada 's first reported vaping-related illness casehttp : **36;25970;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;26008;TOOLONG ... title= " Ontario Minister of Health Christine Elliott has ordered hospitals to begin reporting all cases of vaping-related lung disease to the province 's Chief Medical Officer of Health . " alt= " Ontario Minister of Health Christine Elliott has ordered hospitals to begin reporting all cases of vaping-related lung disease to the province 's Chief Medical Officer of Health . " data-portal-copyright= " Robert F. Bukaty " **28;26044;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1459136 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; A London , Ont. , high school student who ended up in intensive care on life support earlier this month is Canada&amp;rsquo ; s first confirmed case of vaping-related lung illness . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The case underscores growing concern about the health dangers related to an explosion in vaping by high school and elementary school students in Ontario and elsewhere . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Public health officials from London said Wednesday that the youth , who has now recovered , had no other health issues that could have caused the severe illness . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; The only issue that was identified was that the individual vaped e-cigarettes , &amp;rdquo ; said Dr. Chris Mackie , medical officer of health for Middlesex-London . The youth , whose name , age and gender were not identified , vaped &amp;ldquo ; at least daily , &amp;rdquo ; according to Mackie . He wouldn&amp;rsquo ; t say whether the youth vaped marijuana products , or THC , which has been linked to many cases in the U.S. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;h2 class= " cat-head short " **40;26074;TOOLONG ... &lt;ul class= " relatedlinks " &gt; &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href= " https : **33;26116;TOOLONG ... youth with vaping-related illness was on life support : **30;26151;TOOLONG &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s Dr. Andrew Pipe , Canada&amp;rsquo ; s leading expert on smoking cessation , said he was not surprised to hear of the case . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; I think it was just a question of time . I believe there will be more to come as well . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Confirmation of the case comes amid growing reports of vaping related illness and deaths in the U.S. In recent months , officials throughout the U.S. have reported more than 380 cases of vaping related illness , seven of them fatal . Most are youths or young adults . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; This week the U.S. Centers for Disease control and Prevention activated its emergency operations centre to coordinate the investigation into the cases of severe lung disease linked to e-cigarette use . The CDC says it has not yet identified any single vaping product or substance related to the illnesses , although most reported a history of using e-cigarette products containing THC. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Ontario , cases of lung illness suspected of being related to vaping had not been routinely collected until Health Minister Christine Elliott ordered hospitals on Wednesday to begin sending such information to the province&amp;rsquo ; s chief medical officer of health . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In light of the growing evidence , I have become increasingly concerned about the prevalence and possible health consequences of vaping , particularly as they affect our youth , &amp;rdquo ; Elliott said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The case in London , she said , underscores the importance of collecting the information . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; This is the beginning of a crucial stage of collecting data and information that will allow us to fully understand the health risks of vaping , so that we can make informed decisions on how to respond . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Elliott issued an order under the Health Protection and Promotion act requiring public hospitals in the province to provide the chief medical officer of health with non-identifying information about cases of vaping-related severe pulmonary disease . The Public Health Agency of Canada has asked provinces and territories to do so . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Pipe said governments &amp;mdash ; particularly Ontario&amp;rsquo ; s &amp;mdash ; need to do more . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Ontario is probably the worst province in the country in terms of laxity of regulations . It is not as if the government wasn&amp;rsquo ; t warned . Health organizations have consistently warned about e-devices and called for their regulation . &amp;rdquo ; Ontario , unlike many provinces , allows promotion of e-cigarette products in some stores . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In Ottawa , Medical Officer of Health Dr. Vera Etches said she was concerned to hear about the case . &amp;ldquo ; We are going to continue our messaging that vaping is not harmless . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; She said anyone who is vaping should be aware that Health Canada is warning people with any symptoms of serious respiratory illness to seek medical help . Etches said Ottawa Public Health is asking local vaping shops to pass along that warning . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The London case was only identified because a doctor reported it under the Canadian Consumer Safety Act Reporting system , said Mackie. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Mackie , and other public health officials believe there have likely been other cases of illness related to vaping that went unreported because people weren&amp;rsquo ; t looking for them . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If you don&amp;rsquo ; t ask people , you won&amp;rsquo ; t get any reports of illness . In Canada there has been no system , so it is not surprising that no reports have come forward , &amp;rdquo ; Mackie said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The new provincial reporting system , he said , &amp;ldquo ; will really pull the covers back and we will be able to see in a much more detailed way what is happening with illnesses we know it is already causing . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In London , as in Ottawa , vaping is a growing problem among high school and even elementary school students , despite laws banning the sale of e-cigarettes , or their promotion , to minors . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Despite these health protective measures , the use of these products is escalating , especially by high school age youth , which is a major concern for us , &amp;rdquo ; said Mackie. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; A recent study found vaping by youth between 16 and 19 increased by 74 per cent in Canada between 2017 and 2018 . In addition , cigarette smoking by youth has begun to increase after years of declines . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The federal government is considering stricter advertising rules for e-cigarettes . Health Canada , meanwhile , has advised non-smokers , pregnant women and young people not to vape. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;nbsp ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;26183;TOOLONG ... of Westboro bus crash victim Anja Van Beek : We 've lost confidence in OC Transpo and the city&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;26218;TOOLONG ... photo surfaces of Trudeau wearing ' brownface ' costume in 2001&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;26253;TOOLONG ... sought in daytime break-ins in Kanata , Pineglen&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneThu , 19 Sep 2019 09:14:54 **45;26288;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal NewsMenWomenCloutier : In this federal election , let 's talk about funding health infrastructurehttp : **26;26335;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;26363;TOOLONG ... title= " Current federal programs make it difficult to build new hospital infrastructure . " alt= " Current federal programs make it difficult to build new hospital infrastructure . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;26399;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1463291 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Former prime minister Kim Campbell is famously quoted as saying during the 1993 election campaign that &amp;ldquo ; an election is no time to discuss serious issues . &amp;rdquo ; With apologies to Ms. Campbell , election campaigns are excellent ways for Canadians to hold their politicians&amp;rsquo ; feet to the fire on the issues that matter to voters . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Case in point : Canadians will be going to the polls in just over a month and it is high time we heard , from those who seek to represent us in Parliament , how they would actually support innovation and transformation in Canada&amp;rsquo ; s health system in a meaningful way . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; That Canadians actually want federal leadership in health care is a fact that has been proven in poll after poll for decades . The latest evidence is an Angus Reid poll released Aug. 26 that found 71 per cent of uncommitted voters consider improving access to health care their top election issue , a figure that jumps to 80 per cent among Canadians over age 55. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But where to begin ? As a first step , candidates could correct the longstanding oversight of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s federal government to not invest in health care infrastructure or in innovation led by health institutions . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In 2018 , the government of Canada set out an ambitious infrastructure plan calling for investments of more than $180 billion over the course of 12 years . These investments will fund transportation infrastructure , water and wastewater facilities , as well as education and social infrastructure . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Not included on that list ? Health care infrastructure . In fact , hospitals have been explicitly excluded from accessing Infrastructure Canada&amp;rsquo ; s funding programs for more than a decade . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada&amp;rsquo ; s innovation programs &amp;ndash ; whether administered via Innovation Canada or through the Canada Foundation for Innovation ( CFI ) &amp;ndash ; select against leadership by health care providers . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Innovation Canada favours industry leadership , both in its Innovative Superclusters Initiative and through its Strategic Innovation Fund and tends to relegate health care organizations to a supporting role . Meanwhile , CFI requires that any hospital application have sign-off from the president of a university , whose priorities are likely to lie elsewhere . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Why do we do this ? Why do we design these programs in ways that make it either difficult or impossible for hospitals , researchers and patients to benefit ? &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Some may argue that federalism stands in the way ; that anything that happens in a hospital is outside the scope of the federal government , but that is incorrect . In fact , the federal Hospital Construction Grant Program that ran from 1948 to 1970 was the financial engine that built the current hospital infrastructure . Unfortunately , that engine has sputtered to a halt . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canadian voters must hold the federal government responsible for the care and maintenance of the hospitals it helped to create . Many of these institutions are now desperately in need of renewal even as demand increases due to our growing and aging population . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Nearly half of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s hospitals are more than 50 years old and it is impossible to expect 21st-century medicine in a building designed for care models of a half a century ago . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Our aging hospital infrastructure also has the unfortunate side effect of consuming approximately 11 per cent of total public energy and accounting collectively for more than five per cent of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s greenhouse gas footprint . Not only is modern hospital infrastructure important for providing better care , it is also a much greener solution . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In late July , among many other similar announcements , the federal government pledged $1 million to provide much-needed upgrades to 46-year old Blackburn Arena in Ottawa&amp;rsquo ; s east end . Fair enough , but during this election campaign let&amp;rsquo ; s put the puck in the net where it really counts : innovating health care for all Canadians . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; **29;26429;TOOLONG ? mile Cloutier&lt;/strong&gt; is President and CEO of &lt;a href= " http : //www.healthcarecan.ca/ " &gt;Health ... the&lt;span style= " font-family : -apple-system , BlinkMacSystemFont , ' Segoe UI ' , Roboto , Oxygen-Sans , Ubuntu , Cantarell , ' Helvetica Neue ' , sans-serif ; " &gt; national voice of health care organizations and hospitals across Canada . Its goal is to improve the health of Canadians through an evidence-based and innovative healthcare system . &lt;/span&gt;&lt;/em&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;26460;TOOLONG ... a weekend of fun , Ottawa 's light-rail transit system faces busy Monday rush-hour test drive&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;26495;TOOLONG ... Do n't move to the country to build your dream home&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;26530;TOOLONG ... raise questions about Kanata Conservative candidate who once appeared in video with Faith Goldy&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Paul- ? mile CloutierMon , 16 Sep 2019 12:13:25 **45;26565;TOOLONG ... 2019Family &amp;amp ; **33;26612;TOOLONG changer ' : Community health centres lead in building Ottawa health teamhttp : **36;26647;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;26685;TOOLONG ... title= " A stock file photo of a senior woman receiving assistance from a health care worker with a walking frame . " alt= " A stock file photo of a senior woman receiving assistance from a health care worker with a walking frame . " data-portal-copyright= " Daisy-Daisy " **28;26721;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1460777 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; When Ontario overhauls the health system in coming months , Ottawa will buck a provincial trend by putting community health centres , not hospitals , at the centre of the new system . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ottawa Health Team , as it is known , is one of 31 groups across the province that have been given the green light to apply to become some of the province&amp;rsquo ; s first health teams . A coalition of six community health centres are at the centre of the Ottawa proposal , which also includes The Ottawa Hospital , Bruy&amp;egrave ; re , Ottawa Inner City Health , Carefor and Ottawa Public Health , along with numerous other organizations , totalling 47 partners . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Two other Ottawa-area teams have also received green lights to move forward : one focused on children&amp;rsquo ; s health that includes CHEO and more than 50 other members , including Eastern Ontario hospitals ; and one in the city&amp;rsquo ; s east end that includes the Montfort Hospital . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The province announced last spring it would call for groups to form health teams as part of a major revamp of the provincial health system . The Ontario government is in the process of disbanding the LHIN ( local health integration ) system and has concentrated most health organizations in the province into a central agency . LHINs are to be replaced by health teams , which are meant to co-ordinate care among various health organizations . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Health Minister Christine Elliott has said the system will offer &amp;ldquo ; seamless access to health services , including primary care , hospitals , home and community care , palliative care , residential long-term care and mental health and addictions service . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;26751;TOOLONG ... title= " Ontario Health Minister Christine Elliott in a file photo from August . " alt= " Ontario Health Minister Christine Elliott in a file photo from August . " data-portal-copyright= " Tyler Kula " **28;26787;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1460779 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; But critics , including former deputy minister of health Dr. Bob Bell , have raised concerns about the lack of resources and planning for the complex new system and the fact that most teams that have been green-lighted to move ahead are led by hospitals . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Ontario health teams seem at risk for becoming hospital-driven organizations , where the strength of the hospital board governs decisions in the region , &amp;rdquo ; Bell wrote in a recent opinion piece in the Toronto Star . He also wondered whether hospital-driven health teams might be able to &amp;ldquo ; resist the temptation to consolidate regional budgets to gradually provide better hospital funding ? &amp;rdquo ; He noted hospital budgets have been squeezed in recent years . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But those at the centre of planning for the Ottawa Health Team say there is genuine excitement about the prospect of doing things in a different , more streamlined way . Community health centres , which already work with numerous organizations , are well connected , said Simone Thibault , executive director of Centretown Community Health Centre and spokesperson for the project . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; If we are really wanting care to be mainly in the community , we feel that we are well connected . We don&amp;rsquo ; t work in silos . We work with many health and addiction agencies . We also believe that not just the individual should be taken care of , but the whole community . We are about having the right care , at the right time , with the right provider . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Thibault said the team would focus on primary care and preventative care and medicine , as well as acute care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; But the aim of the team to to help fill gaps in the transitions between different types of care , she said . Those transitions often become what Thibault refers to as &amp;ldquo ; pain points . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; As community health centres , if we can be a part of the solution that could address those pain points , then we can do something . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Alan Forster , who is vice-president of innovation and quality at The Ottawa Hospital , said : &amp;ldquo ; Philosophically we are supportive of the region leading this . It is really important for us that the community is leading this and they own it . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Ongoing issues such as the number of alternate level of care patients occupying hospital beds are about &amp;ldquo ; how our community really supports people who are frail , &amp;rdquo ; he said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;26817;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Alan Forster is vice-president of innovation and quality at The Ottawa Hospital . " alt= " Dr. Alan Forster is vice-president of innovation and quality at The Ottawa Hospital . " data-portal-copyright= " The Ottawa Hospital " **28;26853;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1460783 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Trevor Eggleton , manager of communications at Carefor , a not-for-profit charity that provides home care and community support , says his organization is &amp;ldquo ; really excited&amp;rdquo ; about the possibilities of the new system , which he believes will streamline care , inspire innovation and create an environment of collaboration . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are still more questions than answers in what the system built on health teams will eventually look like . Among the looming issues is how teams made up of institutions with various electronic health systems will communicate with each other and share patient records . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Elliott has described it as being a system that puts patients first and moves with them through levels of care . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; For now , the 31 groups involved in trying to set up health teams in Ottawa and around the province are rushing to meet the next deadline &amp;mdash ; Oct. 9 &amp;mdash ; by which time they must have full applications completed . Teams are expected to be announced this fall . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Ottawa Team had 11 initial partners , but more are joining , Thibault said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The CHEO team &amp;mdash ; which has 59 partners &amp;mdash ; is the only health team moving forward under an &amp;ldquo ; innovation category . &amp;rdquo ; It is kicking off a social media campaign asking children and teens to help name it . Joanne Lowe , executive director of the Youth Services Bureau , said the children&amp;rsquo ; s team would focus on the unique needs of youth and children . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;span class= " s1 " &gt; " &lt;/span&gt; I think it speaks volume about how we see kids that we want to be at the front end of this new concept we think it can resolve some issues , &amp;rdquo ; Lowe said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Thibault acknowledged the task of trying to refocus and reorganize the health system is massive , but added : &amp;ldquo ; It is a huge game changer . We thought , if we had an opportunity to make change , we need to embrace it , to pull in our partners and to move it forward . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; epayne@postmedia.com &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;26883;TOOLONG ... add extra joy -- and extra wheel -- to The Ottawa Hospital 's annual Ride&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;26918;TOOLONG ... &lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneMon , 09 Sep 2019 13:57:12 **45;26988;TOOLONG ... &amp;amp ; ChildLocal NewsMenSeniorsWomenWomen more likely to die after bypass surgery : Ontario studyhttp : **35;27035;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;27072;TOOLONG ... title= " Files : Cardiogram monitor in surgery " alt= " Files : Cardiogram monitor in surgery " data-portal-copyright= " Fotolia " **28;27108;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 813971 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; Women are more likely than men to die after cardiac surgery , a study of more than 72,000 Ontario cardiac patients has found . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The study , which was recently published in the Journal of the American Heart Association , found that women were more likely to die than men after coronary artery bypass surgery or valve surgery ( including combined surgeries ) within 30 days as well as in the longer term . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The study , conducted by researchers at the University of Ottawa Heart Institute and the University Health Network in Toronto , adds to mounting evidence that heart disease is different in women than in men and that sometimes women&amp;rsquo ; s health suffers as a result . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Research at the Heart Institute and elsewhere , as well as campaigns for patients and health professionals , have helped raise awareness about this and to avoid heart problems going untreated or being overlooked in women . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Dr. Louise Sun , a cardiac anesthesiologist and clinical scientist at the University of Ottawa Heart Institute , and author of the study , said being female is a &amp;ldquo ; statistically significant risk factor for dying after surgery . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Researchers followed 72,824 Ontario patients who had undergone cardiac bypass and valve surgeries between October 1 , 2008 and December 31 , 2016 . The majority of patients were men &amp;mdash ; reflecting the fact that more men than women suffer from heart disease . Twenty five per cent of the patients were women . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Women had significantly higher rates of death than men after coronary artery bypass surgery and combined bypass surgery and mitral valve surgery . Women had lower rates of long-term mortality after isolated mitral valve replacement , although men had lower death rates after isolated mitral valve repair . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The findings were enough for researchers to find that merely being female is associated with &amp;ldquo ; long-term mortality&amp;rdquo ; after cardiac surgery . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sun pointed to several reasons for women&amp;rsquo ; s high death rates after some cardiac surgery . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One is that women tend to be older than men when they require cardiac bypass surgery , are more frail and more medically complex than men , often with other health issues such as diabetes and hypertension. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In addition , women often experience a delay in treatment compared to men , which means their conditions tend to be more serious when they undergo surgery . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; These established sex differences explain our overall observation of lower estimated long-term survival in women after cardiac surgery and of female sex being an independent risk factor for long-term mortality . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Sun said that among the medical complexities in female patients is the likelihood that some of those undergoing bypass and mitral valve surgery have had a previous heart attack that has been missed . That could explain why they needed surgery to replace a leaky mitral valve . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Although there has been a focus on the need for more research and awareness about women&amp;rsquo ; s heart health in recent years , the study&amp;rsquo ; s results suggest a gap still remains . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Given the substantial sex differences in patient presentation for coronary and valvular heart disease , further efforts need to be directed at the education of both physicians and patients in the early recognition of acute presentation of cardiac disease in women , &amp;rdquo ; the authors wrote . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There is also a need for sex specific risk prediction models for patients undergoing cardiac surgery , something Sun said is in the works . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; While the findings are significant , Sun noted that the risk of dying after bypass surgery remains very low , in the one-per-cent range , &amp;ldquo ; but out of that one per cent we are seeing more women than men . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " **27;27138;TOOLONG " **34;27167;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS : &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;27203;TOOLONG ... critically injured in Orl ? ans dies in Israeli hospital&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;27238;TOOLONG ... girl continues life-saving treatment as third bone marrow registry clinic planned&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;27273;TOOLONG ... called into deaths of Basil Borustki 's three murder victims&lt;/a&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;nbsp ; &lt;/p&gt; Elizabeth PayneThu , 29 Aug 2019 12:59:24 **45;27308;TOOLONG ... NewsWomenCold storage : The promise of Ottawa 's cord blood bankhttp : **36;27355;TOOLONG ... &lt;img src= " https : **34;27393;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Heidi Elmoazzen , atem cell director at Canada Blood Services , in the lab where cord blood donations are processed and stored . " alt= " Dr. Heidi Elmoazzen , atem cell director at Canada Blood Services , in the lab where cord blood donations are processed and stored . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;27429;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1448089 " /&gt; &lt;iframe width= " 100% " height= " 100% " frameborder= " 0 " allowfullscreen= " true " src= " https : **34;27459;TOOLONG ... &gt; &lt;/iframe&gt; &lt;p&gt; Inside two high-tech tanks filled with liquid nitrogen in an industrial park in Nepean there are about 2,500 little bags of frozen stem cells . The contents of each one of these little bags has the potential to save a life . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The &amp;ldquo ; **26;27495;TOOLONG ; freezers contain blood harvested from the umbilical cords and placentas of newborn babies , many of them in Ottawa . The immature stem cells in cord blood are used to treat more than 80 diseases , most frequently blood cancers and other blood disorders such as leukemia , lymphoma , aplastic anemia and sickle cell disease . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are other possible treatments in the pipeline . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; In the future , stem cells might be used to treat a broad range of diseases , from diabetes to Parkinson&amp;rsquo ; s , multiple sclerosis and &amp;mdash ; this is controversial &amp;mdash ; autism . But no , the science does not make it possible to take cord blood and , say , grow a new kidney , said Heidi Elmoazzen , the stem cells director at Canadian Blood Services who has a PhD in cryobiology. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;27523;TOOLONG ... title= " Dr. Heidi Elmoazzen , atem cell director at Canada Blood Services , in the lab where cord blood donations are processed and stored . " alt= " Dr. Heidi Elmoazzen , atem cell director at Canada Blood Services , in the lab where cord blood donations are processed and stored . " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;27559;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1448089 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; The Canadian Blood Services cord blood bank in Nepean is one of three such public banks in Canada ( Quebec has its own ) and preserves about 72 per cent of the agency&amp;rsquo ; s cord blood inventory , with the rest stored in Edmonton . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On any typical day , one to three donations will arrive at the bank&amp;rsquo ; s lab from the birthing unit at the General campus of The Ottawa Hospital , one of four hospitals in Canada where mothers of newborns may opt to donate the cord blood harvested from their newborn&amp;rsquo ; s umbilical cord . Mothers-to-be have to register before the birth to donate and agree again after the birth . The process doesn&amp;rsquo ; t hurt the mother or the baby &amp;mdash ; the umbilical cord is typically treated as medical waste , anyway . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Donors do it for altruistic reasons , to potentially help someone they will never meet . Still , &amp;ldquo ; it&amp;rsquo ; s quite an easy sell , &amp;rdquo ; said Elmoazzen. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are three ways to donate stem cells : through the blood , usually after the donor is given a drug that makes the stem cells exit bone arrow and enter the bloodstream , from the bone marrow itself , typically from the pelvic bone , and through cord blood . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; As transplants go , stem cell transplants are relatively simple . The bag of frozen stem cells , which is stored in a box about the size of an audio cassette tape , is retrieved from the cryopreservation freezer by a robotic arm . Still frozen , tit is transported wherever it is needed in the world inside in a container that looks like a high-tech milk can . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; When it gets to its destination , the unit is thawed . The patient , whose own blood production has been knocked out with chemotherapy , is transfused with the stem cells . If all goes well , within a few weeks the stem cells are repopulating the patient&amp;rsquo ; s body with healthy blood cells . The more closely the donor and the recipient are matched , the less likely the recipient will suffer graft vs. host disease , an immune condition that occurs when the donor&amp;rsquo ; s &lt;span class= " ILfuVd " &gt;&lt;span class= " e24Kjd " &gt;immune cells attack the recipient 's tissues . **26;27589;TOOLONG &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Only about 14 per cent of the total 23,000 donations made in Canada since 2013 have been listed for possible matching . In the lab in Nepean , cord blood is tested for it suitability for donation . There are many reasons why a donation isn&amp;rsquo ; t eligible for transplant . The volume collected or the total nucleated cell count might be too low . The mother might have a health or social history that makes the donation ineligible . Or the cord blood might test positive for a transmissible disease . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The cord blood that does not qualify for donation can , with the mother&amp;rsquo ; s agreement , be used for research in cell therapy and transplant medicine . As of this year , the bank supports 17 cord-blood research projects . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are a lot of moving parts to a complicated international system . Canadian Blood Services made a business case for opening its own cord blood banks . It costs the Canadian health system nothing for a cord blood unit from Canadian Blood Services . It costs about $40,000 U.S. for a unit from outside the country , said Todd Campbell , Canadian Blood Services&amp;rsquo ; business support specialist . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The international exchange of stem cells is a two-way street . About 60 per cent of Canadian donations have gone to recipients in other countries , but 80 per cent of Canadian patients receive stem cells from donors in other countries . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; On the average month , the bank will get 10 to 15 inquiries from transplant physicians around the world about a donation in one of the cord blood freezers . The units are listed in both a Canadian registry and an international database . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Cord blood is often used to treat pediatric patients . One of its advantages is that can be retrieved and delivered very quickly . There are about 440,000 people on Canada&amp;rsquo ; s bone marrow registry , for example , but only about half of those who are approached actually agree to donate if they are requested . The reasons can vary &amp;mdash ; it might have been months or years since a potential donor signed up . The donor might be hard to locate , might have lost interest or have health problems . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada opened its first public cord-blood bank in 2013 . The goal was to create a multi-ethnic cord blood inventory to reflect the diversity of Canada&amp;rsquo ; s population . Since then , Canadian Blood Services has collected blood at four hospitals &amp;mdash ; in Ottawa , Edmonton , Brampton and Vancouver &amp;mdash ; and stores it in two banks , one in Ottawa and the other in Edmonton . The target was to get more than 60 per cent non-Caucasian donors . The bank has already exceeded that target . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; We&amp;rsquo ; re trying to build an inventory of cord blood to be able to transplant more patients in Canada , &amp;rdquo ; said Campbell . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;img src= " https : **34;27617;TOOLONG ... title= " Medical laboratory technician Milenko Drajic checks for the growth of stem cells in a sample at the Nepean lab. " alt= " Medical laboratory technician Milenko Drajic checks for the growth of stem cells in a sample at the Nepean lab. " data-portal-copyright= " Julie Oliver " **28;27653;TOOLONG " 1 " data-license-id= " 1448088 " /&gt; &lt;p&gt; There are also seven private cord blood banks in Canada where parents can opt to store their newborn&amp;rsquo ; s own cord blood . However , there&amp;rsquo ; s a low probability of using one&amp;rsquo ; s own cord blood for transplantation &amp;mdash ; between one in 20,000 and one in 250,000 , according to Health Canada . When a patient needs cord blood transplantation , cells from a healthy donor are preferable if privately banked blood cells carry the same genetic or congenital disorder that caused the disease . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; A lot of people see ( private cord blood banking ) as an insurance policy . But if your child had leukemia , a physician would not use the child&amp;rsquo ; s own cord blood . The genetic mutation is in the stem cells themselves , &amp;rdquo ; said Elmoazzen. &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; The other problem is that a patient sometimes needs more stem cells to be transplanted than those contained in a single cord blood unit . In most cases , even if parents had stored a unit for family use , it would likely not be sufficient for a successful transplantation , and additional units stored in public use banks would still be needed . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; It&amp;rsquo ; s like the dosage of medication . You need a minimum number of stem cells per kilogram of body weight , &amp;rdquo ; said Elmomazzen. &amp;ldquo ; A 20-pound patient needs less than an adult . &amp;rdquo ; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Canada has a target of creating a bank of about 10,000 cord blood units . But that&amp;rsquo ; s a moving target which is re-evaluated regularly and the science evolves . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; So far , 22 cord blood units have been withdrawn the Canadian public bank so far . Why so few ? Transplant surgeons have been looking to source stem cell donations not only from cord blood , but also from adult registries and family members of the patient . Even if a family member is a less-than-perfect match , graft vs. host disease can be managed using pharmaceuticals . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; Meanwhile , the worldwide cord blood supply is growing quickly . There are 780,000 donated units available through the World Marrow Donor Association . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; One of the system&amp;rsquo ; s biggest problems for the system is lack of awareness , said Elmoazzen . But that is changing . When the cord blood bank opened six years ago , about 32 per cent of new mothers consented to donate . That has since increased to 47 per cent . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &amp;ldquo ; In an ideal world , it would like it to be like recycling . Everybody just knows about it and does it , &amp;rdquo ; she said . &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Cord blood by the numbers : &lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;Minus 196 C : &lt;/strong&gt; Temperature at which cord blood is stored in liquid nitrogen &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;23,000 : &lt;/strong&gt; Qualified cord blood units collected since Canada&amp;rsquo ; s public bank launched in 2013 &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;3,300 : &lt;/strong&gt; Cord blood units stored and available for transplant &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;One in four : &lt;/strong&gt; Chance a patient needing a transplant will find a bone marrow donor match in his or her own family &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;$0 : &lt;/strong&gt; Costs to the Canadian medical system when a patient gets a cord blood donation from the Canadian Blood Services&amp;rsquo ; bank &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;$40,000 U.S : &lt;/strong&gt; Approximate cost if cord blood is sourced from elsewhere in the world &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;22 : &lt;/strong&gt; &amp;ldquo ; Withdrawals&amp;rdquo ; from the Canadian Blood Services cord blood bank since it was founded in 2013 &lt;/p&gt; &lt;hr&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;strong&gt;ALSO IN THE NEWS&lt;/strong&gt; &lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt; &lt;a href= " https : **33;27683;TOOLONG ...
